Wave packet decompositions and sharp bilinear estimates for rough Hamiltonian flows
Robert Schippa, Daniel Tataru

Abstract
. The goal of this paper is to prove bilinear L p L^{p} estimates for rough dispersive evolutions satisfying non-degeneracy and transversality assumptions. The estimates generalize the sharp Fourier extension estimates for the cone and the paraboloid. To this end, we require a wave packet decomposition with localization properties in space-time and space-time frequencies. Secondly, we construct a refined wave packet parametrix for dispersive equations with C 1 , 1 C^{1,1} -coefficients by using the FBI transform. As a consequence, we obtain bilinear estimates for solutions to dispersive equations with C 1 , 1 C^{1,1} coefficients provided that the solutions interact transversely.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Bilinear Fourier extension estimates

The purpose of this article is to explore bilinear estimates for solutions to dispersive PDE with rough coefficients under transversality assumptions. These generalize the classical bilinear adjoint Fourier restriction estimates, which correspond to the constant coefficient case, and which we briefly describe now.

The first (sharp up to endpoints) results are due to Wolff
[
44
]
(see
[
6
,
35
]
for earlier results). Formulated for the cone extension operator,

\mathcal{E}_{c}f(x,t)=\int_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|\xi|\sim 1}e^{i(x\cdot\xi+t|\xi|)}f(\xi)d\xi,\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},

Wolff’s bilinear estimate reads

(1.1)

\|\mathcal{E}_{c}f_{1}\mathcal{E}_{c}f_{2}\|_{L^{p}_{t,x}(B_{d+1}(0,R))}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}R^{\varepsilon}\|f_{1}\|_{2}\|f_{2}\|_{2}

for
p=\frac{d+3}{d+1}
with the angular separation condition

(1.2)

\angle(\text{supp}(f_{1}),\text{supp}(f_{2}))\gtrsim 1.

The advent of sharp bilinear estimates had a transformative effect on Fourier restriction theory, whose study was initiated by Stein in the 60s. In particular, the bilinear estimates solve the linear cone restriction problem for
d=3
(
[
44
]
). For
d=4
Ou–Wang
[
26
]
proved the sharp estimates via polynomial partitioning.

The next results were due to Tao
[
33
]
, who showed the corresponding sharp estimates for the paraboloid, for which the extension operator is given by

\mathcal{E}_{\Delta}f(x,t)=\int_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|\xi|\leq 1}e^{i(x\cdot\xi+t|\xi|^{2})}f(\xi)d\xi.

The corresponding bilinear estimate reads

\|\mathcal{E}_{\Delta}f_{1}\mathcal{E}_{\Delta}f_{2}\|_{L^{p}_{t,x}(B_{d+1}(0,R))}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}\|f_{1}\|_{2}\|f_{2}\|_{2}

for
p=\frac{d+3}{d+1}
. Here, due to non-vanishing curvature of the paraboloid, the transversality condition reads

\text{dist}(\text{supp}(f_{1}),\text{supp}(f_{2}))\gtrsim 1.

Henceforth, the bilinear estimates have been widely generalized. We cite works due to Tao
[
34
]
who proved the estimate (
1.1
) at the endpoint
p=\frac{d+3}{d+1}
with
\varepsilon=0
, Vargas
[
42
]
who proved a bilinear restriction estimate for the hyperboloid, and S. Lee
[
23
,
24
]
, who generalized the bilinear estimate to possibly non-elliptic surfaces and oscillatory integral operators. Possibly degenerate surfaces were considered by Lee–Vargas in
[
25
]
; Buschenhenke–Müller–Vargas considered surfaces of finite type
[
9
]
. Bejenaru
[
2
]
provided a unified description in terms of the shape operator.

Since they are
L^{2}
-based, the Fourier extension estimates can be interpreted as bilinear estimates for solutions to PDE. Variants and extensions with precise dependence on the transversality and regularity of the phase are suitable for applications to PDE and have been worked out more recently by Candy
[
10
]
;
see
[
13
,
12
,
11
]
for more applications.

Regarding the cone extension operator, the bilinear Fourier extension estimates can be formulated for
solutions to the half-wave equation:

(1.3)

\|e^{it\sqrt{-\Delta}}f_{1}e^{it\sqrt{-\Delta}}f_{2}\|_{L^{p}_{t,x}(B_{d+1}(0,R))}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}R^{\varepsilon}\|f_{1}\|_{2}\|f_{2}\|_{2}

for
p=\frac{d+3}{d+1}
and
\text{supp}(\hat{f}_{i})
contained in the unit annulus, satisfying the angular separation condition

\angle(\text{supp}(\hat{f}_{1}),\text{supp}(\hat{f}_{2}))\sim 1.

In view of applications, we can also consider solutions to distinct wave equations with quantitatively separated group velocity
|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}|\gtrsim 1
,
\nu_{i}\in(1,2)
; see below. This can replace the assumption on the angular support separation.

1.2.
Wave packet decompositions

A fundamental tool in all modern developments of Fourier restriction theory is the
wave packet decomposition
. This provides a decomposition of the Fourier extension
\mathcal{E}_{\Delta}f
into non-oscillating components, the
wave packets
. This goes back to pioneering work of Fefferman
[
15
]
and Córdoba
[
14
]
with subsequent important contributions by Bourgain
[
4
,
5
,
6
]
. Regarding applications to PDE’s we want to refer to another pioneering work of Fefferman
[
16
]
.

The wave packets are obtained from a maximal simultaneous decomposition in position and frequency space. On a cube
|(x,t)|\lesssim R
, this amounts to an initial spatial decomposition on the scale
\Delta x\sim R^{\frac{1}{2}}
and the frequency scale
\Delta\xi\sim R^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. This decomposition works just as well for
\mathcal{E}_{c}f
, for which we note that due to the flatness of the cone in the radial direction, there are many wave packets with frequency support in different small balls pointing in the same direction. This overlapping can be avoided by
unbalancing the localization scales
[
28
,
44
,
30
,
17
]
, but we do not pursue this idea here.

For the Fourier extension operator of a graph surface
S=\{(\xi,\phi(\xi)):\xi\in A\subseteq B_{d}(0,2)\}
the frequencies remain fixed under time evolution; the position of the wave packet, which is initially centered at
x_{0}
, changes according to the group velocity:
x(T)=x_{0}-t\nabla\phi(\xi_{0})
. Presently, the third and crucial ingredient for the combinatorial argument to prove the bilinear estimates is to take into account the time-frequency localization:
\tau=\phi(\xi_{0})
.

It is the transverse intersection, which significantly reduces the overlap of wave packets. This allows one to go beyond the range of the sharp linear Tomas–Stein
L^{2}
-based estimate, which reads
\|\mathcal{E}_{\Delta}f\|_{L_{t,x}^{\frac{2(d+2)}{d}}(B_{d+1}(0,R))}\lesssim\|f\|_{2}
.

1.3.
Linear and bilinear estimates for solutions to PDEs with rough coefficients

In this paper we look into bilinear estimates for solutions to wave and Schrödinger equations governed by rough coefficients. The question for bilinear estimates for solutions to wave equations with rough coefficients generalizing Wolff’s estimates was raised by Klainerman–Rodnianski–Tao
[
21
, Section 8]
. This is presently answered via wave packet decompositions and bilinear estimates for rough Hamiltonian flows. We begin with formulating an example of our bilinear estimates.

Consider two frequency-localized solutions to the system of wave equations:

(1.4)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\partial_{t}^{2}u_{1}&=\partial_{i}g^{ij}_{1,\ll N}\partial_{j}u_{1}+f_{1},\\
\partial_{t}^{2}u_{2}&=\partial_{i}g^{ij}_{2,\ll N}\partial_{j}u_{2}+f_{2},\end{array}\right.

for
(t,x)\in[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
with
f_{i}\in L_{t}^{1}([0,1];L_{x}^{2})
. We assume that
g^{ij}_{k}\in C^{1,s}
,
k=1,2
,
s\in[0,1]
and that the metrics satisfy the uniform ellipticity condition

(1.5)

\exists\lambda,\Lambda>0:\forall(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{1+d}:\forall\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash 0:\,\lambda|\xi|^{2}\leq g_{k}^{ij}(t,x)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\leq\Lambda|\xi|^{2},\quad k\in\{1,2\}.

Further, in order to guarantee the tranverse propagation of the two waves we require the speed separation condition

|g^{ij}_{k}-\nu_{k}\delta_{ij}|\leq\varepsilon\ll 1,

with
\nu_{1}\sim\nu_{2}\sim 1
, and
|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}|\sim 1
. Above
N\gg 1
denotes the dyadic frequency localization scale. Precisely, we assume that

\text{supp}(\hat{u}_{i})\subseteq\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|\xi|\sim N,\big|\xi/|\xi|-\xi_{*}\big|\leq\varepsilon\},

for some
\xi_{*}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.
We assume from the outset that the metric coefficients are frequency-truncated at frequency
\ll N
, and the admissible error is subsumed in
f_{i}
. This can typically be achieved by commutator arguments, and we refer to the proof of Theorem
5.1
for details.
In summary, here we consider two approximate solutions to wave equations with quantitatively transverse propagation and show the following:

Theorem 1.1

(Bilinear estimates for rough wave equations)
.

Let
u_{i}
be solutions to (
1.4
) as above. Then the following bilinear estimate holds with
p=\frac{d+3}{d+1}

N^{2}\|u_{1}u_{2}\|_{L^{p}_{t,x}([0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}N^{\frac{4}{3+s}\frac{d-1}{d+3}+\varepsilon}\prod_{i=1}^{2}(\|\nabla_{t,x}u_{i}(0)\|_{L^{2}}+N\|f_{i}\|_{L^{1}L^{2}}).

For comparison we record the following estimate for Euclidean wave equations with transversely intersecting cones: Let
u_{1},u_{2}:\mathbb{R}^{1+d}\to\mathbb{C}
denote solutions to constant-coefficient wave equations

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\partial_{t}^{2}u_{1}&=\Delta_{g_{1}}u_{1},\\
\partial_{t}^{2}u_{2}&=\Delta_{g_{2}}u_{2},\end{array}\right.

with
g^{ij}_{k}(x,t)\equiv g^{ij}_{k}
with
|g^{ij}_{k}-\nu_{k}\delta_{ij}|\leq\varepsilon\ll 1
,
\nu_{i}\sim 1
and
|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}|\gtrsim 1
. Here
\Delta_{g}:=\partial_{i}g^{ij}\partial_{j}
.
Then for functions
u_{i}

for frequency-localized at frequency
N

we have the bilinear estimate

\|\nabla_{x}u_{1}\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{2}\|_{L^{\frac{d+3}{d+1}}_{t,x}([0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}N^{\frac{d-1}{d+3}+\varepsilon}\|\nabla_{t,x}u_{1}(0)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\|\nabla_{t,x}u_{2}(0)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}.

This follows in the Euclidean case from writing

u_{i}(t,x)=\cos(t\sqrt{-\Delta_{g_{i}}})u_{i}(0)+\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{-\Delta_{g_{i}}})}{\sqrt{-\Delta_{g_{i}}}}\dot{u}_{i}(0),

multiplying out the solutions to the half-wave equation, rescaling and applying Wolff’s result
[
44
]
, which is known to be sharp up to endpoints.

The estimate in the above theorem goes beyond the range of linear Strichartz estimates (after applying Hölder’s inequality), which can be applied without transversality assumptions. Linear Strichartz estimates for wave equations with rough coefficients were proved by the second author
[
36
,
38
,
39
]
using the FBI transform. The FBI transform decomposes a function into
coherent states
, i.e., Gaussians, which likewise exhaust the uncertainty relation. It goes back to this series of papers that the coherent states propagate along the Hamiltonian flow in phase space:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\dot{x}_{t}&=\frac{\partial a_{N}}{\partial\xi},\\
\dot{\xi}_{t}&=-\frac{\partial a_{N}}{\partial x},\end{array}\right.

where
a_{N}
denotes the Hamiltonian after normalization of frequency to unit scale
(which changes the space-time scale to
R=N
).
But for
C^{1,1}
-coefficients (after paradifferential decomposition), the above Hamiltonian flow is only smooth for times
|t|\lesssim R^{\frac{1}{2}}
. For larger times, we can use the localization properties of the Gaussians.

We now provide a brief overview of our results and of our strategy. Our general bilinear estimates are formulated in Theorems
2.13
and
2.16
, for Hamiltonian flows under low regularity assumptions and conditional upon the existence of a wave packet decomposition. We require the wave packet decomposition to satisfy localization in space, frequency localization, and time-frequency localization,
as described in Assumption
2.5
.
This turns out to be more robust than simply
imposing specific regularity assumptions on the coefficients.

In the second part of the paper we use a modified FBI transform in order to establish a wave packet decomposition for solutions to dispersive PDE’s in the space-time region
|(x,t)|\leq R
governed by symbols
a\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which satisfy the following regularity estimates (see Assumption
4.1
for details):

|\partial_{z}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}a(x,t;\xi)|\lesssim_{\alpha,\beta}\begin{cases}R^{-|\alpha|},&\quad 0\leq|\alpha|\leq 2,\\
R^{-\frac{(|\alpha|-2)_{+}}{2}-2},&\quad|\alpha|\geq 2.\end{cases}

Our wave packet decomposition will be adapted to a lattice
\Lambda_{R}
in phase space with the position and frequency scales
\Delta x\sim R^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
\Delta\xi\sim R^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. Such a decomposition can be constructed globally, but for our purposes we need to
consider data which is phase space localized to a set
\mathcal{Y}
, which has frequencies localized to small balls of size
\nu
, and for an intermediate scale
r
use wave packets associated to enlargement
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}
as in Assumption
2.3
and Definition
2.4
.
The following provides a wave packet decomposition for such phase space localized functions:

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
R\gg 1
, and
\nu\in[R^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta_{0}},1]
. Suppose that
a
is a Hamiltonian, which satisfies Assumption
4.1
, with the set
\mathcal{Y}
satisfying Assumption
2.3
. Let
r\in[\nu^{-2-\delta_{0}},R]
and
\mathcal{Y}_{r},\,\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}
be defined like in Definition
2.4
.
There is a pseudo-differential operator
\chi_{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}}(x,D):L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that for solutions to

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}(D_{t}-a^{w})u&=0,\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1},\\
u(0)&=\chi_{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}}u_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\end{array}\right.

we have the decomposition in
L^{2}
for
t\in[-R,R]
with a rapidly decaying error term

u=\sum_{(x_{0},\xi_{0})\in\Lambda_{r}\cap\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}}\alpha_{(x_{0},\xi_{0})}\phi_{(x_{0},\xi_{0})}(t)+g(t)

with

\|g(t)\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim\nu^{d\big(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}\big)}\text{RapDec}(r\nu^{2})\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\text{ for }2\leq p\leq\infty

and
(\phi_{(x_{0},\xi_{0})})_{(x_{0},\xi_{0})\in\Lambda_{r}\cap\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}}
are normalized wave packets satisfying Assumption
2.5
.

In the bilinear estimates
\nu
will play the role of the transversality parameter. The lower bound
\nu^{-2}
for the time scale is the minimal one for which the wave packet scales are compatible with the
\nu
scale for the frequency localization.

The bounds on
a
imposed above arise naturally as follows. Consider for definiteness a
1
-homogeneous symbol
a(x,t,\xi)=\sqrt{g^{ij}_{\leq N^{\frac{1}{2}}}(x,t)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}}
with coefficients in
C^{1,1}
, and a solution
u
to
(D_{t}-a^{w})P_{N}u=f
. At frequencies
N
the paradifferential decomposition suggests to truncate the coefficients of the metric at frequencies
\leq N^{\frac{1}{2}}
because the incurred error can be estimated perturbatively. After rescaling to unit frequencies we find

a_{N}(x,t;\xi)=\sqrt{g^{ij}_{\leq N^{\frac{1}{2}}}(N^{-1}x,N^{-1}t)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}},

and we see that the regularity estimates from Theorem
1.2
are satisfied with
R=N
.

C^{1,1}
-regularity of the symbols is natural to assume as the flow ceases to be well-defined in general for rougher regularities. We show that basic wave packet properties (see Section
4
) are satisfied when defining packets through conjugation to phase space via the FBI transform and phase space localization.
Similar in spirit, we point out how for wave or Schrödinger equations with
C^{1,s}
-coefficients the Euclidean wave packet decompositions can be recovered on a frequency-dependent time scale.

It is instructive to observe that wave packet decompositions can be regarded as more fundamental than dispersive decay properties. With a wave packet decomposition at hand, which is governed by a
C^{1,1}
-Hamiltonian flow, it is essentially well-known how to infer the same dispersive properties like in Euclidean space provided that the flow satisfies non-degeneracy assumptions like is the case for the Schrödinger or wave evolution. And with the dispersive estimate at hand, Keel–Tao
[
20
]
pointed out how to obtain the endpoint Strichartz estimates via bilinear interpolation arguments. A more robust form of this argument is provided in
[
36
, Theorem 4]
. This
makes the argument applicable to suitable parametrices, not only to exact solution operators.

1.4.
Linear Fourier extension and oscillatory integral estimates

The following remarks on linear (adjoint) restriction estimates are in order.
For the Fourier extension operator these read

(1.6)

\|\mathcal{E}_{x}f\|_{L^{p}_{t,x}(B_{d+1}(0,R))}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}R^{\varepsilon}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}

and are conjectured to hold for

p>\begin{cases}\frac{2(d+1)}{d},\quad&x=\Delta,\\
\frac{2d}{d-1},\quad&x=c.\end{cases}

The conjectural range of
p
hinges on the curvature properties of the surface.

There is a large body of literature
1
1

1

This is by no means exhaustive. We refer also to the references within.

[
5
,
43
,
24
,
3
,
7
,
19
,
27
,
18
]
on
L^{p}
-boundedness properties for oscillatory integral operators with smooth phase functions and amplitude functions

T^{\lambda}f(z)=\int e^{i\phi^{\lambda}(z,\xi)}a^{\lambda}(z,\xi)f(\xi)d\xi.

This clearly generalizes (
1.6
). Above
\phi^{\lambda}(z,\xi)=\lambda\phi(z/\lambda,\xi)
and
a^{\lambda}(z,\xi)=a(z/\lambda,\xi)
;
a,\phi\in C^{\infty}
, and we suppose that the phase function can be regarded as smooth perturbation to the cone or paraboloid. A natural example arises from parametrices of solutions to equations governed by
p\in S^{1}_{1,0}
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}i\partial_{t}u-p(x,t,D)u&=0,\quad(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R},\\
u(0)&=u_{0}.\end{array}\right.

The solution can be represented by a Fourier Integral Operator

u(t,x)=\int e^{i\phi(t,x;\xi)}a(x,t;\xi)\hat{u}(\xi)d\xi+R_{t}u

with an
L^{p}
-bounded error term
R_{t}(x,D)
. The phase function
\phi
solves the eikonal equation

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\partial_{t}\phi(x,t;\xi)+p(x,\nabla_{x}\phi)&=0,\quad(x,t,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
\phi(x,0;\xi)&=\langle x,\xi\rangle.\end{array}\right.

For instance when
p(x,\xi)=\sqrt{g^{ij}(x)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}}
as is the case for solutions to (half-)wave equations on compact Riemannian manifolds, we find that
\phi(x,t;\xi)=\langle x,\xi\rangle-t\sqrt{g^{ij}(x)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}}+\ldots
can be regarded as smooth perturbation of the phase function for a cone extension operator. Moreover, in the translation-invariant case
p(x,t,\xi)=p_{0}(\xi)
the eikonal equation is solved by
\phi(x,t,\xi)=\langle x,\xi\rangle-tp_{0}(\xi)
, which recovers the Fourier extension case.

One may compare the
L^{p}
-estimates for oscillatory integral operators with the similar bounds for the Fourier extension. Oversimplifying matters a bit, it transpired that the multilinear estimates hinging on transversality remained valid as pointed out for bilinear estimates by S. Lee
[
24
]
and for the
d+1
-multilinear estimate by Bennett–Carbery–Tao
[
3
]
.

So did some of the techniques to recover linear from multilinear estimates. In the bilinear case this is accomplished by Whitney decomposition and rescaling; see
[
35
]
. The Bourgain–Guth
[
7
]
argument to infer linear estimates from higher multilinear estimates transpires likewise. But due to the phenomenon of
Kakeya compression
(see
[
43
,
19
]
), that is wave packets clustering in lower-dimensional manifolds, the range of linear estimates for oscillatory integral operators is strictly smaller than for the linear Fourier extension operators.

Indeed, following the multilinear approach to linear estimates, sharp linear estimates for oscillatory integral operators could be proved although the restriction conjecture remains open (see
[
24
,
7
,
19
,
27
]
).

In summary, our contribution can be regarded as first step to examine the ramifications of the more recent developments in multilinear Fourier restriction estimates for dispersive PDEs with rough coefficients. And taking into consideration that the linear estimates for smooth phase functions are strictly worse than in the constant-coefficient case, it is remarkable that the bilinear estimates are retained for
C^{1,1}
-coefficients.

Basic notations:

•

Spatial variables are denoted by
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Time is denoted by
t\in\mathbb{R}
. We let
z=(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
denote space-time. The dual frequency variables are denoted by
(\xi,\tau)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
.

•

For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we denote the Euclidean norm by
|x|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}^{2}
and

B_{d}(y,R)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|x-y|<R\}.

•

For
A,B>0
we write
A\lesssim B
provided that there is a harmless constant (depending on external parameters like dimension, etc)
C\geq 1
such that
A\leq CB
. Similarly, we let
A\sim B
provided that there is
C\geq 1
like above such that
A\leq CB
and
B\leq CA
. More involved dependencies of the constant are indicated by subindices, e.g.,
A\lesssim_{\alpha}B
means that
A\leq C(\alpha)B
for a constant depending on
\alpha
.

•

For
A,B>0
we write
A\ll B
to indicate that
A/B
is a small constant fixed at the outset of the argument.

•

For
R\geq 1
and
F:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
we write
F(R)=\text{RapDec}(R)

provided that for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
there is
C_{N}\geq 1
such that for
R\geq 1
we have

F(R)\leq C_{N}(1+R)^{-N}.

•

Let
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. By
\chi_{A}
we denote the indicator function

\chi_{A}(x)=\begin{cases}1,&\quad x\in A,\\
0,&\quad x\notin A,\end{cases}

and for
r>0
by
\mathcal{N}_{r}(A)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\text{dist}(x,A)<r\}.

•

For
x\in\mathbb{R}
we denote by
x_{+}=\max(x,0)
.

Outline of the paper.
In Section
2
we formulate the bilinear estimates for transversely intersecting curved tubes governed by rough Hamiltonian flows. The bilinear estimates are established in Section
3
through induction-on-scales and combinatorial arguments. In Section
4
we show wave packet decompositions for symbols satisfying decay and regularity estimates relying on the FBI transform. Finally, in Section
5
we apply the wave packet decomposition to recover sharp Strichartz estimates and bilinear estimates for PDEs with rough coefficients.

2.
Bilinear estimates for rough Hamiltonian flows

We formulate the validity of the bilinear estimate in terms of the properties of the Hamiltonian flow. Examples of symbols we consider are

p_{1}(x,t,\xi)=|\xi|_{g}=(g^{ij}(x,t)\xi_{i}\xi_{j})^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad p_{2}(x,t,\xi)=g^{ij}(x,t)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}.

We always require non-degeneracy of the metric and in the first case, where the PDE describes a half-wave evolution, ellipticity of the metric. In the second case the evolution describes a (possibly non-elliptic) Schrödinger equation.

2.1.
Basic regularity assumptions

With applications to quasilinear PDE in mind, we want to consider rough coefficients. More precisely, after a standard paradifferential decomposition, we can assume the coefficients to be smooth, but our bounds shall depend only on as few
x
-derivatives as possible.

Assumption 2.1

(Regularity assumptions on the Hamiltonian at scale
R
)
.

Let
R\gg 1
be a large spatial parameter, and
\nu\in(R^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta_{0}},1]
denote a transversality parameter. We let
\epsilon\ll\nu
.
Let
p\in C_{z,\xi}^{1,1}\cap C^{1}_{z}C^{3}_{\xi}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The mixed regularity is quantified for
|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq 2,\;|\alpha|\geq 1
by

(2.7)

|\partial_{z}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}p(z,\xi)|\leq\epsilon R^{-|\alpha|},\quad(z,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}.

For the regularity in frequency variables we suppose there is
C_{2}\geq 1
such that for
0\leq|\beta|\leq 3
:

(2.8)

|\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}p(z,\xi)|\leq C_{2},\quad(z,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}.

We remark that the regularity assumption (
2.7
) seems fairly strong for
\nu\ll 1
. We comment on the practical applicability, which is leveraged via scaling, of the bilinear estimates at the end of this section.

We let
\overline{C}_{2}=e^{4C_{2}}
and record the following bi-Lipschitz consequence for the bicharacteristics. Introduce the following metric on phase space:

d_{R}((x,\xi),(y,\eta))=R^{-\frac{1}{2}}|x-y|+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|\xi-\eta|.

Lemma 2.2

(Bi-Lipschitz property of bicharacteristics)
.

Let
p\in C_{z,\xi}^{1,1}\cap C_{z}^{1}C_{\xi}^{3}
satisfy Assumption
2.1
. Let
(x_{i}^{t},\xi_{i}^{t})
,
i=1,2
be two bicharacteristics with
(x_{i}^{0},\xi_{i}^{0})\in\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{i}
.
Then it holds for
|t|\leq R
:

d_{R}((x_{1}^{t},\xi_{1}^{t}),(x_{2}^{t},\xi_{2}^{t}))\leq d_{R}((x_{1}^{0},\xi_{1}^{0}),(x_{2}^{0},\xi_{2}^{0}))e^{2C_{2}R^{-1}|t|}.

Proof.

This will be a consequence of Grønwall’s lemma. In the following we suppress a possible explicit time-dependence of the Hamiltonian for brevity. First, we write by the fundamental theorem:

|x_{1}^{t}-x_{2}^{t}|=|x_{1}^{0}-x_{2}^{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\big[\frac{\partial p}{\partial\xi}(x_{1}^{s},\xi_{1}^{s})-\frac{\partial p}{\partial\xi}(x_{2}^{s},\xi_{2}^{s})\big]ds|.

Next, we use the mean value theorem:

\begin{split}\frac{\partial p}{\partial\xi}(x_{1}^{s},\xi_{1}^{s})-\frac{\partial p}{\partial\xi}(x_{2}^{s},\xi_{2}^{s})&=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\partial^{2}p}{\partial x\partial\xi}(x^{s}_{1}+\theta(x_{2}^{s}-x_{1}^{s}),\xi_{1}^{s}+\theta(\xi_{2}^{s}-\xi_{1}^{s}))(x_{2}^{s}-x_{1}^{s})\\
&\quad+\frac{\partial^{2}p}{(\partial\xi)^{2}}(x_{1}^{s}+\theta(x_{2}^{s}-x_{1}^{s}),\xi_{1}^{s}+\theta(\xi_{2}^{s}-\xi_{1}^{s}))(\xi_{2}^{s}-\xi_{1}^{s})ds.\end{split}

We obtain by Minkowski’s inequality:

\begin{split}R^{-\frac{1}{2}}|x_{1}^{t}-x_{2}^{t}|&\leq R^{-\frac{1}{2}}|x_{1}^{0}-x_{2}^{0}|+\epsilon R^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}R^{\frac{-1}{2}}|x_{2}^{s}-x_{1}^{s}|ds+C_{2}R^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}R^{\frac{1}{2}}|\xi_{1}^{s}-\xi_{2}^{s}|ds\\
&\leq R^{-\frac{1}{2}}|x_{1}^{0}-x_{2}^{0}|+C_{2}R^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}d_{R}((x_{1}^{s},\xi_{1}^{s}),(x_{2}^{s},\xi_{2}^{s}))ds.\end{split}

For the frequency component we find by the fundamental theorem:

|\xi_{1}^{t}-\xi_{2}^{t}|=|\xi_{1}^{0}-\xi_{2}^{0}-\int_{0}^{t}\big(\frac{\partial p}{\partial x}(x_{1}^{s},\xi_{1}^{s})-\frac{\partial p}{\partial x}(x_{2}^{s},\xi_{2}^{s})\big)ds\big|.

A further application of the mean-value theorem yields

\frac{\partial p}{\partial x}(x_{1}^{s},\xi_{1}^{s})-\frac{\partial p}{\partial x}(x_{2}^{s},\xi_{2}^{s})=\frac{\partial^{2}p}{(\partial x)^{2}}(x^{*},\xi^{*})(x_{1}^{s}-x_{2}^{s})+\frac{\partial^{2}p}{(\partial x\partial\xi)}(x^{*},\xi^{*})(\xi_{1}^{s}-\xi_{2}^{s}).

By the regularity estimates for
p
and an application of Minkowski’s inequality we find

\begin{split}R^{\frac{1}{2}}|\xi_{1}^{t}-\xi_{2}^{t}|&\leq R^{\frac{1}{2}}|\xi_{1}^{0}-\xi_{2}^{0}|+\epsilon R^{-1}\big(\int_{0}^{t}R^{-\frac{1}{2}}|x_{1}^{s}-x_{2}^{s}|+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|\xi_{1}^{s}-\xi_{2}^{s}|ds\big)\\
&\leq R^{\frac{1}{2}}|\xi_{1}^{0}-\xi_{2}^{0}|+\epsilon R^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}d_{R}((x_{1}^{s},\xi_{1}^{s}),(x_{2}^{s},\xi_{2}^{s}))ds.\end{split}

Taking the estimates together we find

d_{R}((x_{1}^{t},\xi_{1}^{t}),(x_{2}^{t},\xi_{2}^{t}))\leq d_{R}((x_{1}^{0},\xi_{1}^{0}),(x_{2}^{0},\xi_{2}^{0}))+2C_{2}R^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}d_{R}((x_{1}^{s},\xi_{1}^{s}),(x_{2}^{s},\xi_{2}^{s}))ds,

and the claim follows from applying Grønwall’s inequality in integral form.
∎

We shall consider initial data essentially localized to subsets
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{d}
in the phase space, which will be realized via application of a pseudo-differential operator (PDO).
\mathcal{Y}
is supported on a cube of size
R
and in frequencies on a scale
\nu\in(R^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta_{0}},1]
. Later,
\nu
will quantify the transversality of intersecting bicharacteristics. We thicken
\mathcal{Y}
with a margin of size
R\nu
in space and with a margin of
\nu
in frequency; on the scale
r=\nu^{-2}
the symbol of the PDO will be supported on the
(2R\nu,2\nu)
-thickening. At larger scales we can allow for smaller margin, which leads us to sets
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}
. Then, when applying the phase space transform on a scale
r\in[\nu^{-2},R]
, we obtain for the phase space portion on
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}^{c}
an error of
\nu^{d\big(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\big)}\text{RapDec}(r\nu^{2})\|f\|_{2}
. In the following we denote by
\pi_{a}:T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
(x,\xi)\mapsto a
for
a\in\{x,\xi\}
the coordinate projections from phase space.

Assumption 2.3

(Size assumptions on
\mathcal{Y}
)
.

Let
R\gg 1
,
\emptyset\neq\mathcal{Y}\subseteq B_{d}(0,10R)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open set, and
c\ll 1\ll C
. We require that
\mathcal{Y}
satisfies the following size
conditions:

\pi_{\xi}(\mathcal{Y})\subseteq B_{d}(\xi^{*},C\nu).

We consider a thickening
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
which is defined as follows:

\overline{\mathcal{Y}}=\{(y,\eta)\in T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{d}:\;\exists(x,\xi)\in\mathcal{Y}:|y-x|<CR\nu\wedge|\eta-\xi|<C\nu\}.

Suppose that the flow-out of the second thickening
\overline{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}}
under the Hamiltonian flow governed by
p
for
t\in[-10R,10R]

(2.9)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\dot{x}_{t}&=\frac{\partial p}{\partial\xi},\\
\dot{\xi}_{t}&=-\frac{\partial p}{\partial x}\end{array}\right.

is contained in the open set

\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}\supseteq\{(x_{t},t,\xi_{t})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}:(x_{0},\xi_{0})\in\overline{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}},\quad t\in[-10R,10R]\}.

In case
p
is
1
-homogeneous in
\xi
, we can replace the frequency balls with sectors. For the first condition the modified assumption reads for some
\xi^{*}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

\{\xi/|\xi|:\xi\in\pi_{\xi}(\mathcal{Y})\}\subseteq B_{d}(\xi^{*},C\nu).

That
\mathcal{Y}
is spatially contained in an
R
-ball centered at the origin is an assumption to ease notation. Practically, by finite speed of propagation, we can suppose that the solution is localized to balls of size
R
. Since the pointwise regularity assumptions are translation-invariant, the centering at the origin is no loss of generality.

The thickening is motivated by constructing wave packets on the smallest spatial scale
\nu^{-2}
, which corresponds to the largest frequency scale
\nu
. The resolution allows us to observe single wave packets. On the other hand, the spatial thickening
R\nu
is motivated by the metric
d_{R}
on phase space, which balances the spatial and frequency margin; the distance between
\mathcal{Y}
and
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}^{c}
of
R^{\frac{1}{2}}\nu
is propagated up to times
|t|\lesssim R
.

Definition 2.4

(Thickenings of sets in phase space)
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}_{\nu^{-2}}
denote the
(R\nu,\nu)
-thickening, that is a thickening of
R\nu
spatially and
\nu
in frequency, of
\mathcal{Y}
. For a scale
r\in[-\nu^{-2-\delta_{0}},R]
we consider the
(R/r^{\frac{1}{2}-\delta_{0}},r^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta_{0}})
thickening of
\mathcal{Y}_{\nu^{-2}}
denoted by
\mathcal{Y}_{r}
and its thickening
(CR/r^{\frac{1}{2}-\delta_{0}},Cr^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta_{0}})
by
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}
.

Note that for
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}
to be contained in
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
we require
r\geq\nu^{-2-C\delta_{0}}
. Note again that the phase space distance from
\mathcal{Y}_{\nu^{-2}}
to
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}^{c}
is likewise propagated. The sharp localization corresponds to
\delta_{0}=0
. Allowing for
\delta_{0}>0
gives us rapid decay off the localization. Furthermore, when carrying out the induction on scales, we shall also carry out the induction on cubes
Q_{r}
not centered at the origin. This leads us to sets
\mathcal{Y}_{r,t}
, which are obtained from evolving
\mathcal{Y}_{r}
under the Hamiltonian flow, the set
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r,t}
is obtained from taking its
C(Rr^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta_{0}},r^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta_{0}})
thickening. Note that the margin conditions are preserved.

The thickenings come into picture as follows: we consider a pseudo-differential operator which is identically one on
\mathcal{Y}
and supported on
\mathcal{Y}_{\nu^{-2}}
which is well-contained in
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}
for
r\geq\nu^{-2-\delta_{0}^{\prime}}
. Subtracting the wave packets in the whole of
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}
allows us to prove the rapid decay
\text{RapDec}(r\nu^{2})
for the remainder.
When constructing the wave packets in
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
, we consider bicharacteristics emanating from a lattice set. However, for technical reasons we want to control bicharacteristics in an even larger set
\overline{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}}
, which is obtained from thickening
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
once more.

As it turns out, once a flow is defined, most important for the bilinear estimates
will be geometric transversality assumptions for which it will suffice to consider regularity bounds obtained from integrating along the bicharacteristics.

2.2.
Wave packet decompositions

To match the literature on bilinear extension estimates
2
2

2

Another rescaling would lead us to consider the flow on the unit time scale and in the unit cube. Then the wave packets would have a different shape compared to the literature on multilinear estimates, matching instead the body of literature on nonlinear dispersive equations.
, we consider the flow governed by
p(x,t,\xi)
for
|(x,t)|\leq R
. We consider wave packets
\phi_{T_{i}}
on scale
\Delta x\sim R^{\frac{1}{2}}
,
\Delta\xi\sim R^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. More precisely, we introduce a lattice in phase space
\Lambda_{R}=R^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times R^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{Z}^{d}
- the initial data will be decomposed according to this lattice in phase space with
\mathcal{Y}
indicating its
essential
support.

Lattice points, and by extension along the flow,
wave packets
, are labeled by
T\in\Lambda_{R}
. We abuse notation and when the context permits, we also denote the curved cylinder obtained from enlarging the projection of the bicharacteristic also by
T=\{R^{\frac{1}{2}}\pi_{x}(x_{T}(t),\xi_{T}(t)):t\in[0,R]\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
. We formulate the bilinear estimates in terms of wave packets, which satisfy the axioms below:

Assumption 2.5

(Wave packet axioms)
.

Let
1\ll r\leq R
and
(x^{T}_{t},\xi^{T}_{t})
denote a bicharacteristic of the Hamiltonian flow (
2.9
) with
(x_{0}^{T},\xi_{0}^{T})=T
. We require that for any
T\in\Lambda_{r}\cap\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
there is a Schwartz function
\phi_{T}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{1+d})
such that for any
t\in[-r,r]
it holds
|\phi_{T}(z_{1})|\sim|\phi_{T}(z_{2})|
for
|z_{1}-z_{2}|\lesssim r^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
\|\phi_{T}\|_{2}\lesssim 1
.
The functions
\phi_{T}
are required to satisfy the following for any
\delta>0
uniformly in
t\in[-r,r]
:

1.

Localization in position:

\|\chi_{\mathcal{N}^{c}_{r^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta}}(x^{t})}\phi_{T}(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim_{\delta}\text{RapDec}(r),

2.

Localization in frequency:

\|\chi_{\mathcal{N}^{c}_{r^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta}}(\xi^{t})}\widehat{\phi_{T}}(t)\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}}\lesssim_{\delta}\text{RapDec}(r),

3.

Localization in time-frequency:

\|\chi_{\mathcal{N}^{c}_{r^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta}}(-p(x^{t_{0}},t_{0},\xi^{t_{0}}))}\mathcal{F}_{t}[\chi(r^{-\frac{1}{2}}(t-t_{0}))\phi_{T}]\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}}\lesssim_{\delta}\text{RapDec}(r).

Remark 2.6
.

From the essentially constant property it is immediate that in the
r^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta}
-neighborhood of the central bicharacteristic we have the estimate for the amplitude

(2.10)

|\phi_{T}(x,t)|\lesssim r^{-\frac{d}{4}+C\delta},\quad x\in r^{\delta}T.

The following almost orthogonality property is immediate:

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
(\phi_{T})_{T\in\Lambda_{r}\cap\overline{\mathcal{Y}}}
be a family of wave packets, which satisfy Assumption
2.5
. Then for any subcollection
\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\subseteq\Lambda_{r}\cap\overline{\mathcal{Y}}
and
t\in[-r,r]
we have:

\big\|\sum_{T^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}\phi_{T^{\prime}}(t)\big\|_{L_{x}^{2}}\lesssim_{\delta^{\prime}}r^{\delta^{\prime}}(\#\mathbb{T}^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{2}}

for any
\delta^{\prime}>0
.

Practically, the wave packets will constitute solutions to a PDE evolution for initial data localized in phase space.
We make the following assumption, which relates the two-parameter family
(S(t,s))_{t,s}
governing the evolution with the wave packet decomposition. In the following let
Q_{R}
denote a space-time cube of size
R
centered at the origin.

Assumption 2.8

(Frame property for approximate solutions)
.

Let
p
be an Hamiltonian flow satisfying Assumptions
2.1
and
2.3
. Let
(S(t,s))_{t,s\in[-R,R]}
denote the associated two-parameter family of flow maps
S(t,s):L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
which satisfies uniform bounds
\|S(t,s)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 1
and

•

S(t,t)={Id}_{L^{2}}
,

•

S(t_{3},t_{2})S(t_{2},t_{1})=S(t_{3},t_{1})
.

We assume that for any
t\in[-R,R]
there is a family of sets
\big(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r,t}\big)_{r\in[\nu^{-2-\delta_{0}},R]}
,
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{R,t}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r_{2},t}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r_{1},t}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{Y}}\subseteq T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
R\geq r_{2}\geq r_{1}\geq\nu^{-2-\delta_{0}}
,
r\in[\nu^{-2-\delta_{0}},R]
and an associated family of
L^{2}
-bounded pseudo-differential operator
\chi_{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r,t}}(x,D)
. We require that for
t_{i}\in[-R,R]
and
t\in t_{i}+[-r,r]\cap[-R,R]
, and
Q_{r}\subseteq Q_{R}
being a space-time cube of size
r
with time-center
t_{i}
, it holds the expansion in
L^{2}

(2.11)

\begin{split}S(t,t_{i})(\chi_{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r,t_{i}}}f)&=S(t,t_{i})f_{0}+S(t,t_{i})f_{1}\\
&=\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}\subseteq\Lambda_{r}\cap\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r,t_{i}}}\alpha_{T}\phi_{T}(t)+g(t)\text{ with }\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|\alpha_{T}|^{2}\lesssim\|\chi_{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r,t_{i}}}f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\end{split}

with
\#\mathbb{T}\lesssim r^{100d}
and

(2.12)

\|g\|_{L_{x,t}^{p}(Q_{r})}\lesssim\nu^{d\big(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}\big)}\text{RapDec}(r\nu^{-2})\|f\|_{L^{2}},\qquad 2\leq p\leq\infty.

Let
\chi_{\mathcal{Y}}=\chi_{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{R,0}}
. We require for a normalized wave packet
\phi_{T}
at scale
r_{1}\leq r_{2}^{1-\delta}

(2.13)

\|(1-\chi_{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r_{1},t_{f}}}(x,D))\phi_{T}(t_{f})\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim_{\delta}\text{RapDec}(r_{1})

provided that
(x_{T}(t),\xi_{T}(t))
is in the
C(Rr_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta_{0}},r_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta_{0}})
-thickening of
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r_{2},t_{f}}
and
\delta\ll\delta_{0}
chosen small enough.

Remark 2.9
.

The
L^{2}
-estimate for
g
corresponds to
\chi_{\mathcal{Y}}f
in phase space being rapidly decaying off
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}^{c}
. For
r\to\nu^{-2}
the rapid decrease in
r
becomes rapid decay in
r\nu^{2}
.
For the
L^{p}
-estimates we use that the frequencies are essentially localized to a ball of radius
\nu
, which makes Bernstein’s inequality applicable. We comment on the realization of Assumption
2.8
in Subsection
4.4
.

2.3.
Transversality assumptions and bilinear estimates

We seek to obtain the following bilinear estimates for Hamiltonian flows
p_{i}
,
i=1,2
, which are compatible with evolutions
(S_{i}(t,s))
,
i=1,2
;

(2.14)

\big\|S_{1}(t,0)(\chi_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}f_{1})S_{2}(t,0)(\chi_{\mathcal{Y}_{2}}f_{2})\big\|_{L^{\frac{d+3}{d+1}}(Q_{R})}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}R^{\varepsilon}\nu^{-\frac{2}{d+3}}\|f_{1}\|_{L^{2}}\|f_{2}\|_{L^{2}}.

To this end, we require transversality assumptions on the propagation along the Hamiltonian flows
p_{i}
,
i=1,2
.

x_{2}
t
x_{1}

x_{2}
t
x_{1}

Figure 1.
On the left: two transversely interacting wavepackets for Fourier extension operators. The bottoms of the cylinders intersect the
x
-plane in a circle of radius
R^{\frac{1}{2}}
. The direction of the long-side with length
R
is determined by the support
\theta
in Fourier space. On the right: the transverse interaction of curved wave packets. The wave packets retain their size, but the cores become curved.

For translation-invariant flows
p(z,\xi)=p_{0}(\xi)
and
S(t,s)=e^{-i(t-s)p_{0}(\nabla/i)}
the estimates of the form (
2.14
) become the bilinear Fourier extension estimates due to Wolff
[
44
]
and Tao
[
33
]
. Bilinear estimates for oscillatory integral operators with smooth phase functions

T^{\lambda}f(x,t)=\int e^{i\phi^{\lambda}(x,t;\xi)}a^{\lambda}(x,t;\xi)f(\xi)d\xi

and transversality
\nu\sim 1
were proved by Lee
[
24
]
via linearization and following Tao’s argument
[
33
]
(see also
[
4
]
for an earlier contribution). These correspond to smooth flows.

We are now ready to state our results, which
we split into two scenarios depending on the
class of symbols
p
.
We begin with non-degenerate symbols
p_{1},p_{2}
, i.e. when the Hessian
\partial^{2}_{\xi}p_{i}
is nondegenerate, and associated localization regions
\mathcal{Y}_{i}\subset\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{i}
as in Assumption
2.3
. For sufficient control over the bicharacteristics relative to the transversality
\nu
, we confine the frequency support
\pi_{\xi}(\mathcal{Y}_{i})
essentially to a
\nu
-ball. In this case we formulate the following assumptions.

Assumption 2.10

(Transversality assumptions for non-degenerate Hamiltonians)
.

Suppose that
p_{i}\in C_{z,\xi}^{1,1}\cap C^{1}_{z}C^{3}_{\xi}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfies Assumption
2.1
for
i=1,2
on the spatial scale
R\gg 1
with transversality parameter
R^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta_{0}}\leq\nu\leq 1
, and that there are sets
\mathcal{Y}_{i}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{i}\subseteq T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{d}
which satisfy Assumption
2.3
. Suppose that the non-degeneracy assumption is satisfied:

(2.15)

d_{2}\geq|\det(\partial_{\xi}^{2}p_{i}(z,\xi))|\geq d_{1}>0

with
d_{1},d_{2}
uniform in
(z,\xi)\in\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,R}
.

Let
(x_{i}(t),\xi_{i}(t))
be two bicharacteristics governed by
p_{i}
with
(x_{i}(0),\xi_{i}(0))\in\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{i}
for
i=1,2
. Suppose that the bicharacteristics intersect at
t_{I}\in[-R,R]
:
x_{1}(t_{I})=x_{2}(t_{I})
.
We assume the following on
\Delta_{v}=\nabla_{\xi}p_{1}(x_{1}(t),t,\xi_{1}(t))-\nabla_{\xi}p_{2}(x_{2}(t),t,\xi_{2}(t))
for any
t\in[-R,R]
:

(2.16)

\displaystyle|\Delta_{v}|

\displaystyle\sim\nu,

(2.17)

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta_{v},(\partial^{2}_{\xi}p_{j}(x_{j}(t),t,\xi_{j}(t))^{-1}\Delta_{v}\rangle|

\displaystyle\sim\nu^{2}\gtrsim R^{-1+2\delta_{0}}.

Remark 2.11

(Basic transversality)
.

We remark that in case
\nu\sim 1
(
2.17
) implies (
2.16
). Secondly, if
\nu\sim 1
holds, then it suffices to require (
2.17
) at
t=t_{I}
as for
t\in[-R,R]
(
2.17
) persists by the fundamental theorem and regularity of the flow.

Moreover, by the margin condition and the bi-Lipschitz property of the flow, (
2.16
) and (
2.17
) hold also for almost intersecting bicharacteristics:

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
(x_{i}(t),\xi_{i}(t))
be two bicharacteristics governed by
p_{i}
with

(x_{i}(0),\xi_{i}(0))\in\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{i}
for
i=1,2
and the flows satisfy Assumption
2.10
.
Suppose that there is
t_{I}\in[-R,R]
with

|x_{1}(t_{I})-x_{2}(t_{I})|\leq R^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta_{0}}.

Then (
2.16
) and (
2.17
) hold also for the bicharacteristics
(x_{i}(t),\xi_{i}(t))
on
t\in[-R,R]
.

Proof.

In the first step we show that
(x_{1}(t_{I}),t_{I},\xi_{2}(t_{I}))
is in the flow-out of
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}
under
p_{2}
.
To this end, we argue by contradiction. We link
(x_{2}(t_{I}),t_{I},\xi_{2}(t_{I}))
and
(x_{1}(t_{I}),t_{I},\xi_{2}(t_{I}))
with a straight line, parametrized by
\gamma
. Since
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{2,R}
is open, we have

0<t_{*}=\inf(\{t\in[0,1]:\gamma(t)\not\in\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{2,R}\})
. However, for
0<t<t_{*}
we can apply Lemma
2.2
to argue that the pullback
(x_{2}^{\prime}(0),\xi_{2}^{\prime}(0))
along
p_{2}
to
t=0
satisfies
|x_{2}^{\prime}(0)-x_{2}(0)|\leq\overline{C}_{2}R^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta_{0}}
and
|\xi_{2}^{\prime}(0)-\xi_{2}(0)|\leq\overline{C}_{2}R^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta_{0}}
. The estimate is uniform up to
t_{*}
, which gives a contradiction with the margin condition.

Let the intersecting bicharacteristic be denoted by
T_{2}^{\prime}
. Another application of Lemma
2.2
shows that
T_{2}
and
T_{2}^{\prime}
are at any time
t\in[-R,R]
at most separated by
\overline{C}_{2}R^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta_{0}}
in space. The claim then follows from perturbing (
2.16
) and (
2.17
) by a Taylor expansion and regularity of the flow.
∎

Our main bilinear estimate in the scenario of non-degenerate Hamiltonian flows
is as follows:

Theorem 2.13

(Sharp bilinear estimates for non-degenerate flows)
.

Let
R\gg 1
. Suppose that
p_{i}\in C_{z,\xi}^{1,1}\cap C^{1}_{z}C^{3}_{\xi}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfies Assumption
2.10
for sets
\mathcal{Y}_{i}
for
i=1,2
. Suppose that the flows
p_{i}
admit wave packet decompositions, which satisfy Assumption
2.5
, and there are compatible two-parameter group
(S_{i}(t,s))_{t,s\in[-R,R]}
such that Assumption
2.8
is satisfied.

Then the bilinear estimate

(2.18)

\big\|S_{1}(t,0)(\chi_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}f_{1})\,S_{2}(t,0)(\chi_{\mathcal{Y}_{2}}f_{2})\big\|_{L^{\frac{d+3}{d+1}}(Q_{R})}\lesssim C_{\varepsilon}R^{\varepsilon}\nu^{-\frac{2}{d+3}}\|f_{1}\|_{L^{2}}\|f_{2}\|_{L^{2}}

holds true with implicit constants depending on
\epsilon
,
C_{2}
, and
d_{i}
defined in (
2.7
), (
2.8
), and (
2.15
).

We now turn our attention to the second scenario, and formulate a corresponding theorem for
1
-homogeneous symbols
p_{1},p_{2}
. In this case we require the frequencies to be separated from the origin:

Assumption 2.14

(Transversality assumptions for
1
-homogeneous Hamiltonians)
.

Let
1\ll R
be a spatial scale and suppose that
p_{i}\in C_{z,\xi}^{1,1}\cap C^{1}_{z}C^{3}_{\xi}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfies Assumption
2.1
in
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,R}
for
i=1,2
. Suppose that
p_{i}
are
1
-homogeneous in
\xi
, i.e., for
\lambda>0
it holds
p_{i}(x,t,\lambda\xi)=\lambda p_{i}(x,t,\xi)
.

Suppose that the non-degeneracy assumption is satisfied: For
(z,\xi)\in\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,R}
there are
d-1
eigenvectors
w_{1},\ldots,w_{d-1}
for
A=\partial^{2}_{\xi}p(z,\xi)
with eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{d-1}
which satisfy
0<d_{1}\leq|\lambda_{i}|\leq d_{2}
.

Let
(x_{i}(t),\xi_{i}(t))
be two bicharacteristics governed by
p_{i}
with
(x_{i}(0),\xi_{i}(0))\in\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{i}
,
i=1,2
. Suppose that the bicharacteristics intersect for some
t_{I}\in[-R,R]
:
x_{1}(t_{I})=x_{2}(t_{I})
.
We assume the following on
\Delta_{v}=\nabla_{\xi}p_{1}(x_{1}(t),t,\xi_{1}(t))-\nabla_{\xi}p_{2}(x_{2}(t),t,\xi_{2}(t))
for
j\in\{1,2\}
:

(2.19)

\displaystyle|\Delta_{v}|\sim\nu,

(2.20)

\displaystyle|\langle\xi_{j}^{*},\Delta_{v}\rangle|\geq\nu\gtrsim R^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta_{0}}>0.

We have the following analog of Lemma
2.12
about the regularity of almost intersecting bicharacteristics whose proof is omitted to avoid repetition.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
(x_{i}(t),\xi_{i}(t))
be two bicharacteristics governed by
p_{i}
with

(x_{i}(0),\xi_{i}(0))\in\mathcal{Y}_{i}
for
i=1,2
, and the flows satisfy Assumption
2.14
. Suppose that there is
t_{I}\in[-R,R]
with

|x_{1}(t_{I})-x_{2}(t_{I})|\leq R^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta_{0}}.

Then (
2.19
) and (
2.20
) hold also for the bicharacteristics
(x_{i}(t),\xi_{i}(t))
on
t\in[-R,R]
.

Now we can state our main result in this case:

Theorem 2.16

(Sharp bilinear estimates for
1
-homogeneous flows)
.

Let
1\ll R
be a spatial scale and suppose that
p_{i}\in C_{z,\xi}^{1,1}\cap C^{1}_{z}C^{3}_{\xi}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfies Assumption
2.14
for
i=1,2
.
Suppose that the flows
p_{i}
admit wave packet decompositions, which satisfy Assumption
2.5
, and there are compatible two-parameter group
(S_{i}(t,s))_{t,s\in[-R,R]}
such that Assumption
2.8
is satisfied.

Then the bilinear estimate (
2.18
)
holds true with implicit constants only depending on
C_{2}
,
\epsilon
, and
d_{i}
defined in (
2.7
), (
2.8
), and (
2.20
).

We end the section with remarks on the geometric interpretation of the transversality assumptions and the regularity assumption for small transversality.

Remark 2.17

(Geometric interpretation of transversality assumptions)
.

We can also mix the assumptions: Suppose that
p_{1}
is non-degenerate and
p_{2}
is
1
-homogeneous. Then, in addition to the quantitative transversality assumption (
2.16
) we have to require that (
2.17
) holds for
j=1
and (
2.20
) holds for
j=2
. The conditions reflect the following geometric condition on the energy shell
given by

\mathcal{E}^{z}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}=\{\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:p_{1}(z,\xi_{1})+p_{2}(z,\eta+\xi_{2}^{\prime}-\xi_{1})-p_{1}(z,\eta)-p_{2}(z,\xi_{2}^{\prime})=0\}.

Let
A=\partial^{2}_{\xi}p_{1}(z,\eta)
describe the leading order variation of the Hamiltonian flow for a fixed base point along frequencies.
The transversality conditions (
2.17
) and (
2.20
) become instances of

\text{dist}(A\,T_{\eta}\mathcal{E}^{z}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}},\nabla_{\xi}p_{1}(z,\xi_{1})-\nabla_{\xi}p_{2}(z,\xi_{2}))\gtrsim\nu>0.

The regularity assumptions with
\epsilon\ll\nu
allow us to vary the parameters
z
and
\xi_{i}
without changing the condition. We refer to works by Lee–Vargas
[
25
]
, Bejenaru
[
2
]
, and Candy
[
10
]
in the case of constant coefficients.

Remark 2.18

(Transversality vs. regularity)
.

The regularity assumption (
2.7
) with
\epsilon\ll\nu
for small transversality parameter
\nu\ll 1
seems to be a restrictive assumption. We note that in case of self-interactions
p_{1}=p_{2}=p
for wave
p=\sqrt{g^{ij}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}}
or Schrödinger flows
p=g^{ij}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}
with uniformly elliptic
(g^{ij})
the transversality condition on the initial frequencies corresponds to
\nu
-separation for Schrödinger flows and to
\nu
-angle separation for wave flows. In the Schrödinger case, after a Galilean transform and parabolic rescaling we recover unit separation of the frequencies. Correspondingly, in the wave case, after a Lorentz transformation and rescaling, we recover unit angular separation. Notably, the regularity assumptions require additional considerations, and the full discussion is postponed to later work.

3.
Proof of the bilinear estimates for rough Hamiltonian flows

We rely on Tao’s argument
[
33
]
(see also
[
24
]
) to show Theorems
2.13
and
2.16
. The proofs follow the same general strategy:

(1)

We induct on the space-time cubes of size
C\nu^{-2-\delta_{0}}\leq S\leq R
and divide the tubes into focusing and non-focusing part on the coarse scale
R^{1-\delta}
. Roughly speaking, the highly concentrated coarse scales, where the tubes hit the most
R^{\frac{1}{2}}
-cubes after some pigeonholing arguments, can be estimated directly via induction on scales. This takes up Subsections
3.1
-
3.3
.

(2)

It remains to estimate the non-focusing contribution. Here we rely on a biorthogonality estimate. It is obtained from a double counting argument, which differs in the non-degenerate and
1
-homogeneous case. The combinatorial argument is established in Subsection
3.4
.

3.1.
Induction on scales and coarse-scale decomposition

To show the theorems, we induct on
C\nu^{-2-\delta_{0}}\leq r\leq R
. We carry out the induction of uniform bilinear estimates for Hamiltonians satisfying the regularity assumption
2.1
with a parameter
R
, which are associated with the two-parameter groups
(S_{i}(t,s))_{t,s\in[-r,r]}
and associated wave packet decompositions.

Induction Assumption
: For
C\nu^{-2-\delta_{0}}\leq r\leq R/2

we have for a cube
Q_{r}
of size
r
, with time center
t_{i}
, and
Q_{r}\subseteq Q_{R}
and free solutions

(3.21)

\|\prod_{j=1}^{2}S_{j}(t,t_{i})(\chi_{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r,t_{i},j}}(x,D)f_{j})\|_{L^{\frac{d+3}{d+1}}_{t,x}(Q_{r})}\leq C_{\varepsilon}r^{\varepsilon}\nu^{-\frac{2}{d+3}}\|f_{1}\|_{L^{2}}\|f_{2}\|_{L^{2}}.

We shall show in the
induction step
that this implies the above for cubes up to size
2r
. Before we carry out the induction by establishing the
base case
at
r=C\nu^{-2-\delta_{0}}
and the
induction step
to propagate the estimate up to
r=R
, we note how the estimate at
r=R
yields the original claim of Theorems
2.13
and
2.16
by the definition of
\chi_{i}(x,D)
.

We turn to the
base case
, which is dictated by the minimum scale for the validity of wave packet decompositions
r=C\nu^{-2-\delta_{0}}
and still obtaining an admissible error for the wave packet decomposition.
Indeed, this matches that the frequencies are roughly supported on a ball of size
\nu
(or in the homogeneous case on a slab of size
\nu
). In the non-degenerate case we have
\#(\Lambda_{r}\cap\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{i,r})=\mathcal{O}(\nu^{-d\delta_{0}})
. Letting
\delta_{0}=c\varepsilon
for some
c\ll 1
we can assume that the initial frequency remains unchanged, as this incurs only an error of
r^{\varepsilon}
. In the homogeneous case there are possibly more lattice points, but the direction of the frequency does not change essentially.

By Assumption
2.8
we have the wave packet expansion

S_{j}(t,t_{i})\big(\chi_{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r,t_{i},j}}f_{j}\big)=\sum_{T_{j}\in\mathbb{T}_{j}\subseteq\Lambda_{r}\cap\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r,t_{i},j}}a_{T_{j}}f_{j}+S_{j}(t,t_{i})g_{j}

with an error that is negligible on
Q_{r}
.

We pigeonhole the amplitude of the wave packets, which is possible assuming
\|f\|_{2}=1
by linearity and recalling that
\#\mathbb{T}_{j}\leq r^{100d}
by Assumption
2.8
. Next, we recall that the
\ell^{2}
-norm of the amplitude coefficients is bounded by the
L^{2}
-norm of the initial data, and it is enough to show the following estimate for wave packets on the
r
-scale:

\big\|\sum_{T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}}\phi_{T_{1}}\sum_{T_{2}\in\mathbb{T}_{2}}\phi_{T_{2}}\big\|_{L^{\frac{d+3}{d+1}}_{t,x}(Q_{r})}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}r^{\varepsilon}\nu^{-\frac{2}{d+3}}\big(\#\mathbb{T}_{1})^{\frac{1}{2}}\big(\#\mathbb{T}_{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

This will be proved via interpolating between the energy estimate:

\big\|\sum_{T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}}\phi_{T_{1}}\sum_{T_{2}\in\mathbb{T}_{2}}\phi_{T_{2}}\big\|_{L^{1}_{t,x}(Q_{r})}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}r^{1+\varepsilon}\big(\#\mathbb{T}_{1})^{\frac{1}{2}}\big(\#\mathbb{T}_{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}},

which follows from Lemma
2.7
, after applying Hölder’s inequality in time.
Secondly, we show the following based on biorthogonality:

\big\|\sum_{T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}}\phi_{T_{1}}\sum_{T_{2}\in\mathbb{T}_{2}}\phi_{T_{2}}\big\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}(Q_{r})}^{2}\lesssim\nu^{-1}r^{C\delta}r^{-\frac{d-1}{2}}\big(\#\mathbb{T}_{1}\big)\big(\#\mathbb{T}_{2}\big).

We write the left-hand side as

\big\|\sum_{T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}}\phi_{T_{1}}\sum_{T_{2}\in\mathbb{T}_{2}}\phi_{T_{2}}\big\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}(Q_{r})}^{2}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T_{1},T_{1}^{\prime},\\
T_{2},T_{2}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\iint\phi_{T_{1}}\phi_{T_{2}}\overline{\phi_{T_{1}^{\prime}}}\overline{\phi_{T_{2}^{\prime}}}dxdt.

Since the initial frequency (or in the homogeneous case: the initial frequency direction) is always the same, for tubes
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
there are only
\lesssim r^{C\delta}
tubes
T_{1}^{\prime}
and
\lesssim r^{C\delta}
tubes
T_{2}^{\prime}
such that

\iint\phi_{T_{1}}\phi_{T_{2}}\overline{\phi_{T_{1}^{\prime}}}\overline{\phi_{T_{2}^{\prime}}}dxdt

is not rapidly decreasing in
r
. As a consequence of Lemma
2.2
, the above expression can only be essentially contributing provided that the initial positions of
T_{1}
,
T_{1}^{\prime}
and
T_{2}
,
T_{2}^{\prime}
are not apart by
\gtrsim r^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta}
.

By an application of Cauchy-Schwarz, we find

\begin{split}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T_{1},T_{1}^{\prime},\\
T_{2},T_{2}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\iint\phi_{T_{1}}\phi_{T_{2}}\overline{\phi_{T_{1}^{\prime}}}\overline{\phi_{T_{2}^{\prime}}}dxdt&\leq r^{C\delta}\sum_{T_{1},T_{2}}\iint|\phi_{T_{1}}|^{2}|\phi_{T_{2}}|^{2}dxdt\\
&\quad+\text{RapDec}(r)\big(\#\mathbb{T}_{1}\big)\big(\#\mathbb{T}_{2}\big).\end{split}

We can now carry out the estimate by computing the maximum essential overlap of
\phi_{T_{1}}
,
\phi_{T_{2}}
as
r^{\delta}\big((r^{\frac{1}{2}})^{d}\times(\nu^{-1}r^{\frac{1}{2}})\big)
, using the transversality, and taking into account the amplitude size bounded by
r^{-\frac{d}{4}+C\delta}
:

\iint|\phi_{T_{1}}|^{2}|\phi_{T_{2}}|^{2}dxdt\lesssim r^{-d}\nu^{-1}r^{\frac{1}{2}}r^{\frac{d}{2}}r^{C\delta}.

This finishes the proof of the base case.

We turn to the induction step
r/2\to r
, establishing (
3.21
) on a cube of size
r
, taking the estimate (
3.21
) at
r/2
for granted. We rely again on Assumption
2.8
yielding the wave packet decomposition

S_{j}(t,t_{i})\big(\chi_{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r,t_{i},j}}f_{j}\big)=\sum_{T_{j}\in\mathbb{T}_{j}\subseteq\Lambda_{r}\cap\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r,t_{i},j}}a_{T_{j}}f_{j}+S_{j}(t,t_{i})g_{j}

with an error that is negligible on
Q_{r}
and pigeonhole the amplitudes
|a_{T_{j}}|\sim\alpha
, which incurs only a logarithmic loss.

Decompose
Q_{r}=\bigcup Q_{i,r^{1-\delta}}
modulo zero measure sets into a disjoint family of cubes with sidelength
r^{1-\delta}
. The collection is denoted by
\mathcal{S}_{1-\delta}
. We will choose
\delta=\varepsilon/C
for a large constant
C
depending on the regularity parameters.

We have by Minkowski’s inequality:

\big\|\sum_{T_{1}}\phi_{T_{1}}\sum_{T_{2}}\phi_{T_{2}}\big\|_{L^{\frac{d+3}{d+1}}(Q_{r})}\leq\sum_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{1-\delta}}\big\|\sum_{T_{1}}\phi_{T_{1}}\sum_{T_{2}}\phi_{T_{2}}\big\|_{L^{\frac{d+3}{d+1}}(S)}.

For tubes
T_{i}\in\mathbb{T}_{i}
and cubes
S\in\mathcal{S}_{1-\delta}
, through a series of pigeonholing arguments, we will below define a relation
T_{i}\sim S
that broadly says that
T_{i}
is concentrated on
S
. We split the highly concentrated contribution of tubes
T_{i}\sim S
(see Definition
3.1
) and the contribution of tubes, which are not concentrated on
S
:

(3.22)

\begin{split}&\quad\big\|\sum_{T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}}\phi_{T_{1}}\sum_{T_{2}\in\mathbb{T}_{2}}\phi_{T_{2}}\big\|_{L^{\frac{d+3}{d+1}}(S)}\leq\underbrace{\big\|\sum_{T_{1}\sim S}\phi_{T_{1}}\sum_{T_{2}\sim S}\phi_{T_{2}}\big\|_{L^{\frac{d+3}{d+1}}(S)}}_{(I)}\\
&\quad+\underbrace{\big\|\sum_{T_{1}\not\sim S}\phi_{T_{1}}\sum_{T_{2}\sim S}\phi_{T_{2}}\big\|_{L^{\frac{d+3}{d+1}}(S)}}_{(II)}+\underbrace{\big\|\sum_{T_{1}\sim S}\phi_{T_{1}}\sum_{T_{2}\not\sim S}\phi_{T_{2}}\big\|_{L^{\frac{d+3}{d+1}}(S)}}_{(III)}\\
&\quad+\underbrace{\big\|\sum_{T_{1}\not\sim S}\phi_{T_{1}}\sum_{T_{2}\not\sim S}\phi_{T_{2}}\big\|_{L^{\frac{d+3}{d+1}}(S)}}_{(IV)}.\end{split}

The contribution of
(I)
is estimated by induction on scales and the key property of the focusing relation:

(3.23)

\forall T_{i}\in\mathbb{T}_{i}:\#\{S\in\mathcal{S}_{1-\delta}:T_{i}\sim S\}\lesssim\log(r)^{c}.

Let
t_{S}
be the time-center of
S
. Applying the induction assumption to estimate
(I)
at scale
r^{1-\delta}
deserves clarification as we only have it at scale
r^{1-\delta}
for products of functions
S_{j}(t,t_{S})\chi_{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r^{1-\delta},t_{S},j}}f_{j}
localized with the PDO
\chi_{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r^{1-\delta},t_{S},j}}
. By the assumption on the phase space localization of the wave packets we have

\|(1-\chi_{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r^{1-\delta},t_{S},j}})\phi_{T}(t_{S})\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim_{\delta}\text{RapDec}(r)

for
(x_{T}(t_{S}),\xi_{T}(t_{S}))
contained in the
C(Rr^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta_{0}},r^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta_{0}})
thickening of

{\mathcal{Y}}_{r,t_{S},j}
.
This condition is satisfied for the wave packets initated at
t_{i}
like above, by the bi-Lipschitz property of the flow; see also Subsection
4.4
.

By the polynomial bound on
\#\mathbb{T}_{j}
we have in
L^{2}
for
t\in t_{S}+[-r^{1-\delta},r^{1-\delta}]
and the bound on

\sum_{T_{j}\in\mathbb{T}_{j}}\phi_{T_{j}}(t)=S_{j}(t,t_{S})\chi_{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r,t_{S},j}}(x,D)\big(\sum_{T_{j}\in\mathbb{T}_{j}}\phi_{T_{j}}(t)\big)+\text{RapDec}(r)

In summary, we have

\begin{split}\big\|\sum_{T_{1}\sim S}\phi_{T_{1}}\sum_{T_{2}\sim S}\phi_{T_{2}}\big\|_{L^{\frac{d+3}{d+1}}(S)}&\leq\big\|\prod_{j=1}^{2}S_{j}(t,t_{S})\chi_{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r^{1-\delta},t_{S},j}}\big(\sum_{T_{j}\sim S}\phi_{T_{j}}(t_{S})\big)\big\|_{L^{\frac{d+3}{d+1}}(S)}\\
&\quad+\text{RapDec}(r).\end{split}

This can now be estimated by the induction hypothesis and Lemma
2.7
:

\begin{split}&\quad\big\|\prod_{j=1}^{2}S_{j}(t,t_{S})\chi_{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r^{1-\delta},t_{S},j}}\big(\sum_{T_{i}\sim S}\phi_{T_{j}}(t_{S})\big)\big\|_{L^{\frac{d+3}{d+1}}(S)}\\
&\leq C_{\varepsilon}r^{\varepsilon(1-\delta)}\nu^{-\frac{2}{d+3}}C_{\delta^{\prime}}r^{\delta^{\prime}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\#\{\mathbb{T}_{j}\sim S\}^{\frac{1}{2}}.\end{split}

Coming back to (
3.22
): Using the induction hypothesis, Cauchy-Schwarz, changing the order of summation and (
3.23
), we find up to rapid decay

\begin{split}(I)&\lesssim_{\varepsilon,\delta^{\prime}}r^{(1-\delta)\varepsilon+\delta^{\prime}}\nu^{-\frac{2}{d+3}}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{1-\delta}}\#\{T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}:T_{1}\sim S\}^{\frac{1}{2}}\#\{T_{2}\in\mathbb{T}_{2}:T_{2}\sim S\}^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\lesssim_{\varepsilon,\delta^{\prime}}r^{(1-\delta)\varepsilon+\delta^{\prime}}\nu^{-\frac{2}{d+3}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\big(\sum_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{1-\delta}}\#\{T_{i}\in\mathbb{T}_{i}:T_{i}\sim S\}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\lesssim_{\varepsilon,\delta^{\prime}}r^{(1-\delta)\varepsilon+\delta^{\prime}}\nu^{-\frac{2}{d+3}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\big(\sum_{T_{i}\in\mathbb{T}_{i}}\#\{S\in\mathcal{S}_{1-\delta}:T_{i}\sim S\}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\lesssim_{\varepsilon,\delta^{\prime}}r^{(1-\delta)\varepsilon+\delta^{\prime}}\nu^{-\frac{2}{d+3}}\log(r)^{c}(\#\mathbb{T}_{1})^{\frac{1}{2}}(\#\mathbb{T}_{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}.\end{split}

So, induction closes for the highly concentrated part as a consequence of the property (
3.23
) of the focusing relation, choosing
\delta^{\prime}
small and
r
large enough.

In the following we again denote
r
by
R
to ease notation. It will suffice to prove an estimate for the non-concentration part
(II)
:

\big\|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1},\\
T_{1}\not\sim S\end{subarray}}\phi_{T_{1}}\sum_{T_{2}\in\mathbb{T}_{2}}\phi_{T_{2}}\big\|_{L^{\frac{d+3}{d+1}}(S)}\lesssim R^{C\delta}\nu^{-\frac{2}{d+3}}(\#\mathbb{T}_{1})^{\frac{1}{2}}(\#\mathbb{T}_{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}.

By symmetry it suffices to consider
(II)
; the estimate of
(III)
is carried out analogously.
(IV)
can be estimated either like
(II)
or
(III)
.

3.2.
Pigeonholing and the focusing relation

We turn to the estimate of the non-concentrated parts
(II)
and
(III)
. By symmetry it suffices to estimate
(II)
.
To make sense of these terms, we need
to define the focusing relation, which will be achieved via several pigeonholings.

Decompose
Q_{R}
into essentially disjoint cubes
q
of side length
R^{\frac{1}{2}}
, the collection being denoted by
\mathfrak{q}
. Now let for
1\leq\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\leq R^{100d}
,
\mu_{i}\in 2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
:

\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]=\{q\in\mathfrak{q}:\#\{T_{i}\in\mathbb{T}_{i}:q\cap R^{\delta}T_{i}\neq\emptyset\}\sim\mu_{i}\}.

Next we pigeonhole tubes from
\mathbb{T}_{1}
. For
\lambda_{1}\in 2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
\lambda_{1}\leq R^{100d}
let

\mathbb{T}_{1}[\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]=\{T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}:\#\{q\in\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]:R^{\delta}T_{1}\cap q\neq\emptyset\}\sim\lambda_{1}\}.

Definition 3.1

(The relation
\sim
between tubes and cubes)
.

Let
S\in\mathcal{S}_{1-\delta}
and for
T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}[\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]
we let
T_{1}\sim_{\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}S
if the number of cubes
q\in\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]
,
q\subseteq S
with
R^{\delta}T_{1}\cap q\neq\emptyset
is maximal. If there are several
S\in\mathcal{S}_{1-\delta}
, choose anyone of them. With
S
fixed, we let moreover
T_{1}\sim_{\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}S^{\prime}
for
S
and
S^{\prime}
neighouring cubes.
We let
T_{1}\sim S
if
T_{1}\sim_{\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}S
for some
\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
.

Remark 3.2
.

The relation
\sim
satisfies the counting relation (
3.23
) because there are
\lesssim\log(R)^{3}
choices for dyadic numbers
1\leq\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\leq R^{100d}
.

It suffices to estimate on
Q_{R}

(3.24)

\begin{split}&\quad\big\|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1},\\
T_{1}\not\sim\mathbb{T}_{1}\end{subarray}}\phi_{T_{1}}\sum_{T_{2}\in\mathbb{T}_{2}}\phi_{T_{2}}\big\|_{L^{\frac{d+3}{d+1}}(S)}\\
&\leq\sum_{1\leq\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\lambda_{1}\leq R^{100d}}\big\|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}[\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}],\\
T_{1}\not\sim T_{1}\end{subarray}}\phi_{T_{1}}\sum_{T_{2}}\phi_{T_{2}}\big\|_{L^{\frac{d+3}{d+1}}(\bigcup\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]\cap S)}\end{split}

For
T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}[\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]
with
T_{1}\sim_{\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}S

we have by averaging

(3.25)

\#\{q\in\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]:q\subseteq S,\quad q\cap R^{\delta}T_{1}\neq\emptyset\}\gtrsim\lambda_{1}R^{-c\delta}.

We turn to the estimate of the non-focusing part after pigeonholing, i.e., fixing the maximizing dyadic values
\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
in (
3.24
), which incurs a permissible logarithmic loss:

(3.26)

\big\|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T_{1}\not\sim S,\\
T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}[\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]\end{subarray}}\phi_{T_{1}}\sum_{T_{2}}\phi_{T_{2}}\big\|_{L^{\frac{d+3}{d+1}}(\mathcal{Q}=\bigcup\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]\cap S)}\lesssim R^{C\delta}(\#\mathbb{T}_{1})^{\frac{1}{2}}(\#\mathbb{T}_{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}.

The estimate (
3.26
) will follow from interpolating the energy estimate and an estimate obtained from biorthogonality. The energy estimate does not depend on the pigeonholing and is given by:

(3.27)

\big\|\sum_{T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}}\phi_{T_{1}}\sum_{T_{2}}\phi_{T_{2}}\big\|_{L^{1}(S)}\lesssim R^{1-\delta}R^{\delta^{2}}(\#\mathbb{T}_{1})^{\frac{1}{2}}(\#\mathbb{T}_{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}.

This is based on the almost orthogonality of the wave packets at
t>0
by Lemma
2.7
, after applying Hölder’s inequality.

The second estimate is a consequence of biorthogonality, which proof relies on the pigeonholing and focusing relation:

(3.28)

\begin{split}\big\|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T_{1}\not\sim S,\\
T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}[\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]\end{subarray}}\phi_{T_{1}}\sum_{T_{2}}\phi_{T_{2}}\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{Q})}&\leq\sum_{q\in\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]\cap S}\sum_{T_{1},T_{2},T_{1}^{\prime},T_{2}^{\prime}}\iint_{q}\chi_{q}^{4}\phi_{T_{1}}\phi_{T_{2}}\overline{\phi_{T_{1}^{\prime}}}\overline{\phi_{T_{2}^{\prime}}}dxdt\\
&\lesssim\nu^{-1}R^{C\delta}R^{-\frac{d-1}{2}}(\#\mathbb{T}_{1})(\#\mathbb{T}_{2}).\end{split}

where
\chi_{q}
denotes a smooth bump function adapted to
q
, which we assume for simplicity to be of product type, such that

\chi_{q}\geq c>0\text{ on }q,\quad\text{supp}(\chi_{q})\subseteq 2q,\text{ and }\sum_{q}\chi_{q}^{4}\equiv 1.

Interpolating (
3.27
) with (
3.28
) at
p=\frac{d+3}{d+1}
yields (
3.25
). The remainder of the argument consists of establishing (
3.28
).

3.3.
Fine-scale decomposition and conservation laws

We start by investigating when
T_{1},T_{2},T_{1}^{\prime},T_{2}^{\prime}
make a significant contribution on
q\in\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]
to the integral in (
3.28
). Recall that
T_{1},T_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}[\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]
and
T_{1},T_{1}^{\prime}\notin S
. To derive the conservation laws, we for the moment omit this information from notation, nor is it relevant to this point.

Let
I_{t,q}=\pi_{t}(q)
denote the
R^{\frac{1}{2}}
-interval of times covered in
q
and
t_{q}=c(I_{t,q})
denote the center in time. Let
x_{q}=c_{x}(q)
denote the center in space coordinates and
z_{q}=(x_{q},t_{q})
the space-time center.

We write

\iint_{B_{d+1}(0,R)}\phi_{T_{1}}(z)\overline{\phi_{T_{1}^{\prime}}(z)}\phi_{T_{2}}(z)\overline{\phi_{T_{2}^{\prime}}(z)}dz=\sum_{q\in\mathcal{Q}_{R^{\frac{1}{2}}}}\iint\chi^{4}_{q}\phi_{T_{1}}(z)\overline{\phi_{T_{1}^{\prime}}(z)}\phi_{T_{2}}(z)\overline{\phi_{T_{2}^{\prime}}(z)}dz

The cubes
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{R^{\frac{1}{2}}}
for which

\iint\chi_{q}^{4}\phi_{T_{1}}(z)\overline{\phi_{T_{1}^{\prime}}(z)}\phi_{T_{2}}(z)\overline{\phi_{T_{2}^{\prime}}(z)}dz=\text{RapDec}(R)

are referred to as
inessential
as these can be neglected in the proof of the estimate, recalling that we always have a bound in
\#T_{j}
that is polynomial in
R
.
In the following we collect identities, which must hold true, else the cube will be inessential.
We obtain

(3.29)

\begin{split}&\;\iint_{\bigcup\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]\cap S}\big|\sum_{T_{1}\not\sim S}\phi_{T_{1}}\big|^{2}\big|\sum_{T_{2}}\phi_{T_{2}}\big|^{2}\\
&\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\in\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}],\\
q\subseteq S\end{subarray}}\iint\chi_{q}^{4}\big|\sum_{T_{1}\not\sim S}\phi_{T_{1}}\big|^{2}\big|\sum_{T_{2}}\phi_{T_{2}}\big|^{2}\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T_{1},T_{1}^{\prime}.\\
T_{2},T_{2}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\in\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}],\\
q\subseteq S\end{subarray}}\iint\chi_{q}^{4}\phi_{T_{1}}\overline{\phi_{T_{1}^{\prime}}}\phi_{T_{2}}\overline{\phi_{T_{2}^{\prime}}}\\
&=\sum_{T_{i},T_{j}^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\in\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}],\\
\text{essential }q\subseteq S\end{subarray}}\iint\chi_{q}^{4}\phi_{T_{1}}\overline{\phi_{T_{1}^{\prime}}}\phi_{T_{2}}\overline{\phi_{T_{2}^{\prime}}}+\text{RapDec}(R)\big(\#\mathbb{T}_{1}\big)\big(\#\mathbb{T}_{2}\big).\end{split}

For
t\in I_{T}
we can suppose the following identities:

Congruence of position:

(3.30)

\begin{split}x_{T_{1}}(t)+\mathcal{O}(R^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta})&=x_{T_{1}^{\prime}}(t)+\mathcal{O}(R^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta})=x_{T_{2}}(t)+\mathcal{O}(R^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta})\\
&\quad=x_{T_{2}^{\prime}}(t)+\mathcal{O}(R^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta}).\end{split}

Conservation of momentum:

(3.31)

\xi_{T_{1}}(t)+\xi_{T_{2}}(t)=\xi_{T_{1}^{\prime}}(t)+\xi_{T_{2}^{\prime}}(t)+\mathcal{O}(R^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta}).

To prove the above two statements, we argue by contradiction and suppose that the congruence of position or conservation of momenta fails at one time
t\in I_{t,q}
. For this time-slice, we have

\int\phi_{T_{1}}(t,x)\overline{\phi_{T_{1}^{\prime}}(t,x)}\phi_{T_{2}}(t,x)\overline{\phi_{T_{2}^{\prime}}(t,x)}dx=\text{RapDec}(R).

We shall see that in this case the identities are violated for all times
t\in I_{t,q}
. But then the contribution over the whole cube becomes negligible. Here we use that position and momentum are not significantly changing over
q
. This can be justified by linearizing the Hamiltonian flow on
q
.

Lemma 3.3
.

For
t\in I_{t,q}
we have

|x_{t}-(x_{t_{q}}+(t-t_{q})\frac{\partial p}{\partial\xi}(x_{t_{q}},t_{q},\xi_{t_{q}}))|\lesssim 1\text{ and }|\xi_{t}-\xi_{t_{q}}|\lesssim R^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

In particular, if (
3.30
) or (
3.31
) are violated, then it holds

\iint\chi_{q}^{4}\phi_{T_{1}}(t,x)\overline{\phi_{T_{1}^{\prime}}(t,x)}\phi_{T_{2}}(t,x)\overline{\phi_{T_{2}^{\prime}}(t,x)}dxdt=\text{RapDec}(R).

Proof.

Suppose
t_{q}=0
to ease notation. We have by Taylor expansion:

\begin{split}x_{t}&=x_{0}+t\frac{\partial p}{\partial\xi}(x_{0},0,\xi_{0})+\mathcal{O}(t^{2}\big(\frac{\partial^{2}p}{\partial\xi\partial x}\frac{\partial p}{\partial\xi}-\frac{\partial^{2}p}{\partial\xi^{2}}\frac{\partial p}{\partial x}\big)),\\
\xi_{t}&=\xi_{0}+\mathcal{O}(t\frac{\partial p}{\partial x}).\end{split}

We estimate both terms by (
2.7
) and (
2.8
):

\big|t^{2}\big(\frac{\partial^{2}p}{\partial\xi\partial x}\frac{\partial p}{\partial\xi}-\frac{\partial^{2}p}{\partial\xi^{2}}\frac{\partial p}{\partial x}\big)\big|\lesssim_{C_{2}}\epsilon.

For the second term we have

\big|t\frac{\partial p}{\partial x}\big|\lesssim_{C_{2}}\epsilon R^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

This shows that the Hamiltonian flow is not essentially changing over
q
. Then, it follows from the wave packet axioms concerning the localization in position and frequency that violation of (
3.30
) leads to rapid decay. If (
3.31
) is violated, the claim follows from Plancherel’s theorem.
∎

The third conservation law is the
conservation of energy
:

(3.32)

p_{1}(z_{q},\xi_{T_{1}}(t_{q}))+p_{2}(z_{q},\xi_{T_{2}}(t_{q}))=p_{1}(z_{q},\xi_{T_{1}^{\prime}}(t_{q}))+p_{2}(z_{q},\xi_{T_{2}^{\prime}}(t_{q}))+\mathcal{O}(R^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta}),

which is a consequence of time orthogonality.

Lemma 3.4
.

Suppose that (
3.32
) fails. Then

\big|\iint_{q}\chi_{q}^{4}\phi_{T_{1}}\overline{\phi_{T_{1}^{\prime}}}\phi_{T_{2}}\overline{\phi_{T_{2}^{\prime}}}dz\big|\lesssim\text{RapDec(R)}.

Proof.

We apply Plancherel’s theorem in time

\iint\chi_{q}\phi_{T_{1}}\overline{\chi_{q}\phi_{T_{1}^{\prime}}}\chi_{q}\phi_{T_{2}}\overline{\chi_{q}\phi_{T_{2}^{\prime}}}dz=\iint\langle\mathcal{F}_{t}[\chi_{q}\phi_{T_{1}}]*\mathcal{F}_{t}[\chi_{q}\phi_{T_{2}}],\mathcal{F}_{t}[\chi_{q}\phi_{T_{1}^{\prime}}]*\mathcal{F}_{t}[\chi_{q}\phi_{T_{2}^{\prime}}]\rangle dx.

By using the essential support of the time frequencies according to Assumption
2.5
(3) we can suppose that

\begin{split}&\big|p_{1}(x_{T_{1}}(t_{q}),t_{q},\xi_{T_{1}}(t_{q}))+p_{2}(x_{T_{2}}(t_{q}),t_{q},\xi_{T_{2}}(t_{q}))\\
&\quad-p_{1}(x_{T^{\prime}_{1}}(t_{q}),t_{q},\xi_{T^{\prime}_{1}}(t_{q}))-p_{2}(x_{T^{\prime}_{2}}(t_{q}),t_{q},\xi_{T^{\prime}_{2}}(t_{q})\big|\lesssim R^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta}.\end{split}

Now by the congruence of position and Taylor expansion we conclude

|p_{1}(z_{q},\xi_{T_{1}}(t_{1}))+p_{2}(z_{q},\xi_{T_{2}}(t_{q}))-p_{1}(z_{q},\xi_{T_{1}^{\prime}}(t_{q}))-p_{2}(z_{q},\xi_{T_{2}^{\prime}}(t_{q}))|\lesssim R^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta}.

∎

To summarize, at this point we have determined that all essential contributions to
the quadrilinear expression in (
3.29
) must satisfy the conservation of
position (
3.30
), momentum (
3.31
) and energy (
3.32
). This allows us to restrict our attention only to such quadruples of tubes, which we now seek to describe.
By momentum conservation we have for essential cubes

\xi_{T_{2}}(t_{q})=\xi_{T_{1}^{\prime}}(t_{q})+\xi_{T_{2}^{\prime}}(t_{q})-\xi_{T_{1}}(t_{q})+\mathcal{O}(R^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta})

and by Taylor expansion, we can plug this identity into (
3.32
).

Fixing two of the tube frequencies, this leads us to define the energy difference function, as a function of a third frequency
\eta
,

F^{z}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}(\eta)=p_{1}(z,\xi_{1})+p_{2}(z,\eta+\xi_{2}^{\prime}-\xi_{1})-p_{1}(z,\eta)-p_{2}(z,\xi_{2}^{\prime}),

where
z=(x,t)
,
\xi_{1}\in\Xi
,
\xi_{2}^{\prime}\in\Xi^{\prime}
and
T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}
such that
|x_{T_{1}}(t)-x|\lesssim R^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta}
.

With this notation, the set of frequencies
\eta
which yields essential contributions
is confined to an
energy shell
, defined as the collection of frequencies of
T_{1}
-tubes at time
t_{q}
, which essentially annihilate the energy difference function:

\mathcal{E}^{q}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}=\{\eta_{T_{1}}(t_{q}):T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1},\;|F^{z_{q}}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}(\eta_{T_{1}}(t_{q}))|\lesssim R^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta}\}.

For
q\in\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]
let

\mathbb{T}_{1}^{\not\sim S}(q,\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
denote the tubes
T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}[\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]
associated to
q
, i.e. with
T_{1}\not\sim S
,
R^{\delta}T_{1}\cap q\neq\emptyset
.
Given frequencies
(\xi_{1},\xi^{\prime}_{2})
, let
\mathbb{T}_{1}^{\not\sim S}(\mathcal{E}^{z_{q}}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}},\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
be the subset of tubes
T_{1}

whose spatial frequency
\eta_{T_{1}}(t_{q})
at
t_{q}=c_{t}(q)
belongs to the associated energy shell

|F^{z_{q}}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}(\eta_{T_{1}}(t_{q}))|\lesssim R^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta}.

We find from counting and integration, taking into account the wave packet amplitude size in (
2.10
):

(3.33)

\begin{split}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\in\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]\\
q\subseteq S\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T_{i},T_{j}^{\prime},\\
T_{1}\not\sim S,T_{1}^{\prime}\not\sim S\end{subarray}}\iint\chi_{q}^{4}\phi_{T_{1}}\overline{\phi_{T_{1}^{\prime}}}\phi_{T_{2}}\overline{\phi_{T_{2}^{\prime}}}dz&\lesssim R^{-\frac{d-1}{2}}R^{c\delta}\sum_{q}|\mathbb{T}_{1}^{\not\sim S}(q,\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})|\\
&\,\times|\mathbb{T}_{2}(q)|\,\big(\sup_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}\big|\mathbb{T}_{1}^{\not\sim S}(\mathcal{E}^{q}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}},\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\big|\big).\end{split}

The following combinatorial estimate for
q\in\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]
,
q\subseteq S
is decisive:

(3.34)

|\mathbb{T}_{1}^{\not\sim S}(\mathcal{E}^{q}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}},\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})|\lesssim\nu^{-1}R^{c\delta}\frac{\#\mathbb{T}_{2}}{\lambda_{1}\mu_{2}}.

Here
q,\xi_{1},\xi^{\prime}_{2}
are fixed, and the fact that we are counting tubes not concentrated on
S
is essential.

If this is the case we can continue (
3.33
) as follows:

(3.35)

\begin{split}\eqref{eq:AuxEstimateEnergyI}&\lesssim R^{-\frac{d-1}{2}}\sum_{q\in\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]}|\mathbb{T}^{\not\sim S}_{1}(q,\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})|R^{c\delta}\nu^{-1}\frac{\#\mathbb{T}_{2}}{\lambda_{1}\mu_{2}}|\mathbb{T}_{2}(q)|\\
&\lesssim R^{c\delta}\nu^{-1}\#\mathbb{T}_{2}\sum_{q\in\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]}\frac{|\mathbb{T}^{\not\sim S}_{1}(q,\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})|}{\lambda_{1}}.\end{split}

Finally, we find from changing the summation:

(3.36)

\begin{split}\sum_{q\in\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]}|\mathbb{T}_{1}^{\not\sim S}(q,\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})|&\leq\sum_{T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}[\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]}\#\{q\in\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]:R^{\delta}T_{1}\cap q\neq\emptyset\}\\
&\lesssim\lambda_{1}|\mathbb{T}_{1}|,\end{split}

where the requirement that we are only counting tubes not concentrated on
S
is nonessential and is dropped.
Plugging (
3.36
) into (
3.35
) we conclude the proof of the estimate (
3.28
).

3.4.
The combinatorial estimate

Here we lay the last brick in the proofs of Theorems
2.13
and
2.16
by establishing (
3.34
), which we recall here:

(3.37)

|\mathbb{T}_{1}^{\not\sim S}(\mathcal{E}^{q}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}},\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})|\lesssim R^{c\delta}\nu^{-1}\frac{\#\mathbb{T}_{2}}{\lambda_{1}\mu_{2}}.

For this we critically use the non-focusing property of
T_{1}
from the above set. Firstly, observe that for any such
T_{1}
there are
\gtrsim R^{-c\delta}\lambda_{1}
cubes
q^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]
with
R^{\delta}T_{1}\cap q^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
which are at distance
\gtrsim R^{1-\delta}
from
q\subseteq S
because
T_{1}
is not concentrating on
S
. By pigeonholing there are roughly
\mu_{2}
tubes
T_{2}\in\mathbb{T}_{2}
passing through
q^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]
with
\text{dist}(q^{\prime},q)\geq R^{1-\delta}
.
Recall the estimate (
3.25
) from averaging, which gives

\begin{split}&|\{(q^{\prime},T_{1})\in\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]\times\mathbb{T}_{1}^{\not\sim S}(\mathcal{E}^{q}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}},\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):\;\text{dist}(q^{\prime},q)\gtrsim R^{1-\delta},R^{\delta}T_{1}\cap q^{\prime}\neq\emptyset\}|\\
&\qquad\qquad\gtrsim R^{-C\delta}\lambda_{1}|\mathbb{T}_{1}^{\not\sim}(\mathcal{E}^{q}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}},\lambda_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})|.\end{split}

Consequently, adding tubes
T_{2}
through
q^{\prime}
to the counting, we have

(3.38)

\begin{split}&\quad|\{(q^{\prime},T_{1},T_{2})\in\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]\times\mathbb{T}_{1}^{\not\sim S}(\mathcal{E}^{q}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}},\lambda,\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\times\mathbb{T}_{2}:q^{\prime}\cap R^{\delta}T_{1}\neq\emptyset,\\
&\quad\text{dist}(q^{\prime},q)\geq R^{1-\delta},\;q^{\prime}\cap R^{\delta}T_{2}\neq\emptyset\}|\gtrsim R^{-C\delta}\lambda_{1}\mu_{2}|\mathbb{T}_{1}^{\not\sim S}(\mathcal{E}^{q}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}},\lambda,\mu_{1},\mu_{2})|.\end{split}

In order to change the counting order on the left, we now fix
T_{2}\in\mathbb{T}_{2}
, and we estimate the size of the set

\begin{split}\mathcal{Q}_{q}[\lambda,\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]&=\{(q^{\prime},T_{1})\in\mathfrak{q}[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]\times\mathbb{T}_{1}^{\not\sim S}(\mathcal{E}^{q}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}},\lambda,\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):\text{dist}(q,q^{\prime})\geq R^{1-\delta},\\
&\quad R^{\delta}T_{2}\cap q^{\prime}\neq\emptyset,\;R^{\delta}T_{1}\cap q\neq\emptyset,\;T_{1}\cap R^{\delta}q^{\prime}\neq\emptyset\}.\end{split}

We claim that

(3.39)

|\mathcal{Q}_{q}[\lambda,\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]|\lesssim\nu^{-1}R^{C\delta}.

Suppose that (
3.40
) holds. Then we can estimate
