lhs\eqref{eq:ProductSetCounting}\lesssim\nu^{-1}R^{C\delta}|\mathbb{T}_{2}|,

which yields the desired bound (
3.37
).

At this point the symplifying observation is that
the parameters
\lambda,\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
as well as the nonconcentration on
S
no longer play a role. Dropping
these constraints we define the larger and simpler set

\begin{split}\mathcal{Q}_{q}&=\{(q^{\prime},T_{1})\in\mathfrak{q}\times\mathbb{T}_{1}(\mathcal{E}^{q}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}):\text{dist}(q,q^{\prime})\geq R^{1-\delta},\\
&\quad R^{\delta}T_{2}\cap q^{\prime}\neq\emptyset,\;R^{\delta}T_{1}\cap q\neq\emptyset,\;T_{1}\cap R^{\delta}q^{\prime}\neq\emptyset\}.\end{split}

and replace (
3.39
) with the stronger bound

(3.40)

|\mathcal{Q}_{q}|\lesssim\nu^{-1}R^{C\delta}.

It remains to prove (
3.40
). This is where we use the transversality condition, and therefore the proofs of Theorems
2.13
and
2.16
bifurcate.

3.4.1.
Proof of Theorem
2.13

In this section we deal with the non-degenerate case, i.e., the Hessians of
p_{j}
are assumed to be non-degenerate.

We have the following lemma as a consequence of the non-degeneracy of the Hamiltonian flow, which reduces us to count the intersections
q^{\prime}
with
T_{2}
and
T_{1}
. The lemma states that only
R^{c\delta}
tubes from an energy shell can pass through two
R^{1-\delta}
separated points.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
q^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{q}
such that there is
T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}
so that
(q^{\prime},T_{1})\in\mathcal{Q}_{q}
. Let

\mathcal{Q}_{q}(q^{\prime})=\{T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}(\mathcal{E}^{q}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}):(q^{\prime},T_{1})\in\mathcal{Q}_{q}\}.

Then it holds

|\mathcal{Q}_{q}(q^{\prime})|\lesssim R^{c\delta}.

Proof.

We shall see that
T_{1}
is essentially determined by
q
and
q^{\prime}
as property of bi-Lipschitz flow.
Note that the cubes
q
and
q^{\prime}
are separated on a scale of
R^{1-\delta}
in time (because we have the bound
|\partial_{\xi}p_{i}|\lesssim 1
and the tubes could not hit both cubes otherwise).

For the
p_{1}
bicharacteristic starting at
x_{0}
with frequency
\xi_{0}
we have the Taylor expansion

x(t)=x_{0}+t\frac{\partial p_{1}}{\partial\xi}(x_{0},\xi_{0})+t^{2}\zeta(t,x_{0},\xi_{0}).

For the remainder of the Taylor expansion we have the following Lagrange representation:

\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)\big[\frac{\partial^{2}p_{1}}{\partial x\partial\xi}(x_{s},\xi_{s})\frac{\partial p_{1}}{\partial\xi}(x_{s},\xi_{s})-\frac{\partial^{2}p_{1}}{\partial\xi^{2}}(x_{s},\xi_{s})\frac{\partial p_{1}}{\partial x}(x_{s},\xi_{s})\big]ds=t^{2}\zeta(t,x_{0},\xi_{0})

with
\zeta
a continuous function and bounds only depending on
\varepsilon
and
C_{2}
.

We compare two bicharacteristics starting at the same point but with different frequencies, at times
R^{1-\delta}\leq|t|\leq R
:

\begin{split}x_{1}(t)&=x_{0}+t\frac{\partial p_{1}}{\partial\xi}(x_{0},\xi_{1})+t^{2}\zeta(t,x_{0},\xi_{1}),\\
x_{2}(t)&=x_{0}+t\frac{\partial p_{1}}{\partial\xi}(x_{0},\xi_{2})+t^{2}\zeta(t,x_{0},\xi_{2}).\end{split}

By the non-degeneracy of the Hessian we have

|t|\big|\frac{\partial p_{1}}{\partial\xi}(x_{0},\xi_{1})-\frac{\partial p_{1}}{\partial\xi}(x_{0},\xi_{2})\big|=|t||\partial^{2}_{\xi}p_{1}(x_{0},\xi^{*})(\xi_{1}-\xi_{2})|\geq|t|d_{1}C_{2}|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|.

Since
p\in C^{1,1}_{z,\xi}\cap C^{1}_{z}C^{3}_{\xi}
we have

|\zeta(t,x_{0},\xi_{1})-\zeta(t,x_{0},\xi_{2})|\lesssim\frac{\epsilon|t|^{2}}{R}|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|.

Consequently, for
\epsilon
small enough, we find

|x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t)|\geq\frac{C_{2}d_{1}|t|}{2}|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|.

With
\xi_{i}\in\mathcal{L}^{*}
separated by
R^{-\frac{1}{2}}
this bounds the numbers of frequencies resulting in tubes which can intersect
q
after a time
|t|\gtrsim R^{1-\delta}
, by
R^{c\delta}
.
∎

With the previous lemma at hand, it suffices to estimate the intersections of
q^{\prime}
for which
\mathcal{Q}_{q}(q^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
with
T_{2}
. With the next lemma, we can bound the cubes
q^{\prime}
as follows.

Lemma 3.6
.

It holds

\big(\bigcup_{q^{\prime}:\mathcal{Q}_{q}(q^{\prime})\neq\emptyset}q^{\prime}\big)\cap T_{2}\subseteq T_{2}|_{I_{\tau}}

with
I_{\tau}
being a time interval of size
\nu^{-1}R^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta}
.

With the last two lemmas at hand, the desired bound (
3.40
) immediately follows. It remains to prove this last lemma.

Proof.

Recall that the energy shell is given by

\mathcal{E}^{q}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}=\{\eta\in\Xi:\,|p_{1}(z_{q},\xi_{1})+p_{2}(z_{q},\eta+\xi_{2}^{\prime}-\xi_{1})-p_{1}(z_{q},\eta)-p_{2}(z_{q},\xi_{2}^{\prime})|\lesssim R^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta}\}.

We define the zero energy shell
\mathcal{E}^{q}_{0,\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}=\{\eta\in\Xi:\,F^{z_{q}}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}(\eta)=0\}
where it suffices to consider bicharacteristics
(x(t),\xi(t))
with
x(t_{q})=x_{q}
and
F^{z_{q}}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}(\xi(t_{q}))=0
. Note that for the energy difference functional,
by the transversality condition we know that its gradient
\nabla_{\xi}F=\nabla_{\xi}(p_{2}(z_{q},\eta+\xi_{2}^{\prime}-\xi_{1})-p_{1}(z_{q},\eta))
is of size
\gtrsim\nu
and it is normal to the energy shell. This allows us to estimate the thickness of the energy shell (in normal direction by
\nu^{-1}R^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta}
. For this reason the variation of a bicharacteristic taking into account the thickness of the shell can be estimated by

(3.41)

R^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta}\nu^{-1}|\frac{\partial x}{\partial\eta}|\lesssim R^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta}\nu^{-1},

which makes it acceptable to restrict to the zero enery shell
F^{z_{q}}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}(\eta)=0
as this is precisely the scale of
T_{2}\big|_{I_{\tau}}
.

Without loss of generality
we will assume that
0=t_{q}<t_{q^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{I}_{T}=[\frac{R^{1-\delta}}{10},R]
. The restriction on the time interval stems from the timewise difference between
q
and
q^{\prime}
.
For
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
we consider the
d-1
-dimensional manifold

\Gamma_{q}(t)=\{(x(t),t):(x(t),\xi(t))\text{ is a bicharacteristic of }p_{1},\;x(t_{q})=x_{q},\;F(\xi(t_{q}))=0\}

describing the time slice of the spatial projection of the tubes passing through
q
and with frequency in the energy shell. We let

\Gamma_{q}=\bigcup_{t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}}\Gamma_{q}(t),

which is a
d
-dimensional manifold.

It will suffice to show that any intersection of
\Gamma_{q}
with
T_{2}
occurs with a transversality of at least
\nu
. The following facts come together:

•

We have restricted the frequencies to
F^{z_{q}}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}(\xi(t_{q}))=0
.
Below are further remarks on varying
\Gamma_{q}
along the frequencies.

•

We can estimate the number of total intersections of
\Gamma_{q}
with
T_{2}
by
\mathcal{O}(1)
. This will be verified at the end of the proof.

We take an intersection point
(x_{t_{2}},t_{2})
of
T_{2}
with
\Gamma_{q}
. Let
\eta_{1}
denote the “intersection frequency”, which satisfies
\eta_{1}\in\mathcal{E}^{z_{q}}_{0,\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}
and
(x_{t_{2}},t_{2})\in T_{1}(z_{q},\eta_{1}).
We examine the tangent space of
\Gamma_{q}
at this point.

We obtain
d-1
spatial tangent vectors
\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{\Gamma_{q}},\ldots,\mathfrak{t}_{d-1}^{\Gamma_{q}}
of
\Gamma_{q}
from varying the frequencies in
T_{\eta_{1}}\mathcal{E}^{z_{q}}_{0,\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}=\langle\mathfrak{t}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{t}_{d-1}\rangle
, and observing the variation of the Hamiltonian flow
\frac{\partial x_{t_{q}}}{\partial\eta}
at
q
. For
i=1,\ldots,d-1
we obtain

\mathfrak{t}_{i}^{\Gamma_{q}}=\frac{\partial x_{t_{q}}}{\partial\xi}\mathfrak{t}_{i}.

This is complemented with the tangential direction of the intersecting tube
T_{2}
. Note that here we use the fact that we are working on a scale of
R^{\frac{1}{2}}
(up to admissible losses of
R^{c\delta}
).

Let
\mathfrak{t}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{t}_{d-1}
denote an orthonormal basis of the tangent space
T_{\eta_{1}}\mathcal{E}^{z_{q}}_{0,\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}
at
\eta_{1}
(corresponding to an intersection frequency of
\Gamma_{q}(t)
with
T_{2}
). Note that an (unnormalized) normal of
T_{\eta_{1}}\mathcal{E}^{z_{q}}_{0,\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}
is given by

\mathfrak{n}_{\mathcal{E}^{z_{q}}_{0}}=\nabla_{\xi}[p_{2}(z_{q},\eta_{1}+\xi_{2}^{\prime}-\xi_{1})-p_{1}(z_{q},\eta_{1})]=\nabla_{\xi}p_{2}(z_{q},\xi_{2}(t_{q}))-\nabla_{\xi}p_{1}(z_{q},\xi_{1}(t_{q})).

As a consequence of the transversality assumption we have
|\mathfrak{n}_{\mathcal{E}^{z_{q}}_{0}}|\gtrsim\nu
.

We compute the variation of
(x_{\eta_{1}+\varepsilon\mathfrak{t}_{i}}(t),t)
at
\varepsilon=0
with
\eta_{1}\in\mathcal{E}^{z_{q}}_{0,\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}
and
x_{\eta}(t)
denoting the spatial projection of the bicharacteristic governed by
p_{1}
passing through
(x(t_{q})=x_{q},\eta)
at
t=t_{q}
. To this end, we compute the variation of the Hamiltonian flow with respect to frequencies:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\dot{x}_{t}&=\frac{\partial p_{1}}{\partial\xi}(x_{t},t,\xi_{t}),\\
\dot{\xi}_{t}&=-\frac{\partial p_{1}}{\partial x}(x_{t},t,\xi_{t}).\end{array}\right.

Taking the derivative with respect to
\xi
we find

\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial x_{t}}{\partial\xi}=\frac{\partial^{2}p_{1}}{\partial\xi\partial x}(x_{t},\xi_{t})\frac{\partial x_{t}}{\partial\xi}+\frac{\partial^{2}p_{1}}{(\partial\xi)^{2}}\frac{\partial\xi_{t}}{\partial\xi}.

Note that
\partial_{\xi}x_{t=t_{q}}=0
because the bicharacteristics have the common starting point
x_{q}
. We obtain by integration and projection to
\mathfrak{t}_{i}
:

\frac{\partial x_{t}}{\partial\xi}\mathfrak{t}_{i}=\int_{t_{q}}^{t}\frac{\partial^{2}p_{1}}{\partial\xi\partial x}(x_{s},s,\xi_{s})\frac{\partial x_{s}}{\partial\xi}\mathfrak{t}_{i}ds+\int_{t_{q}}^{t}\frac{\partial^{2}p_{1}}{(\partial\xi)^{2}}(x_{s},s,\xi_{s})\frac{\partial\xi_{s}}{\partial\xi}\mathfrak{t}_{i}ds.

The first integral is estimated as
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon R^{-1}(t-t_{q})^{2})
, the second integral is
\mathcal{O}(t-t_{q})
. Here we use

\big|\frac{\partial x_{t}}{\partial\xi}\big|\leq|t-t_{q}|\text{ and }\big|\frac{\partial\xi_{t}}{\partial\xi}-I_{d}\big|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{R}|t-t_{q}|

as an immediate consequence of the regularity of the Hamiltonian
p_{1}
and integration
in the linearized Hamilton flow.

Consequently, upon normalization denoted by
(\cdot)_{\mathbf{n}}
, we find

\big(\frac{\partial x_{t}}{\partial\xi}\mathfrak{t}_{i}\big)_{\mathbf{n}}=\frac{1}{t-t_{q}}\int_{t_{q}}^{t}\frac{\partial^{2}p_{1}}{\partial\xi^{2}}ds\,\mathfrak{t}_{i}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon R^{-1}(t-t_{q})).

Recall that
\varepsilon\ll\nu
and we aim to show a transversality of order
\nu
. This means an error of order
\varepsilon
is negligible. We let

A=\frac{1}{t_{q^{\prime}}-t_{q}}\int_{t_{q}}^{t_{q^{\prime}}}\frac{\partial^{2}p_{1}}{\partial\xi^{2}}(x_{s},s,\xi_{s})ds,

which by non-degeneracy assumption on
p
can be regarded as normalized.
Then it suffices to check
\nu
-transversality using the
\varepsilon
-approximate spatial tangent vectors of
\Gamma_{q}(t)
:

\big(A\mathfrak{t}_{i},0),\quad i=1,\ldots,d-1.

To complete the tangent space to
\Gamma_{q}
we add one last tangent vector which also points in the time direction. By
\eta_{1}(t)
we denote the frequency evolution of the bicharacteristic
(x(t),\xi(t))
with
(x(t_{q}),\xi(t_{q}))=(x_{q},\eta_{1})
.
The derivative in
t
yields a tangent vector of
\Gamma_{q}
with non-trivial time component:

V_{1}=(\frac{\partial p_{1}}{\partial\xi}(z_{q^{\prime}},\eta_{1}(t_{q^{\prime}})),1).

On the other hand the tangent vector of
T_{2}
is given by

V_{2}=(\frac{\partial p_{2}}{\partial\xi}(z_{q^{\prime}},\eta_{2}(t_{q^{\prime}})),1).

Hence for the desired transversality condition we need to prove the space-time relation

(3.42)

|\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{\Gamma_{q}}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathfrak{t}_{d-1}^{\Gamma_{q}}\wedge V_{1}\wedge V_{2}|\gtrsim\nu,

which is in turn equivalent to the purely spatial relation

|\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{\Gamma_{q}}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathfrak{t}_{d-1}^{\Gamma_{q}}\wedge(V_{1}-V_{2})|\gtrsim\nu.

It suffices to check that the difference of
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
is quantitatively transverse to the spatial tangential vectors. We substitute
\mathfrak{t}_{j}^{\Gamma_{q}}

by
A\mathfrak{t}_{j}
as discussed above. Inverting
A
(which is allowed due to the non-degeneracy of
\partial^{2}_{\xi}p_{1}
(
2.15
)), this is equivalent to

|\mathfrak{t}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathfrak{t}_{d-1}\wedge A^{-1}\big(\nabla_{\xi}p(z_{q^{\prime}},\eta_{1}(t_{q^{\prime}}))-\nabla_{\xi}p(z_{q^{\prime}},\eta_{2}(t_{q^{\prime}}))|\gtrsim\nu.

To this end, we use the normalized normal vector of
\mathcal{E}^{z_{q}}_{0,\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}
given by
\mathfrak{n}_{\mathcal{E}_{0}^{z_{q}}}/\nu
and write

A^{-1}\big(\nabla_{\xi}p_{1}(z_{q^{\prime}},\eta_{1}(t_{q^{\prime}}))-\nabla_{\xi}p_{2}(z_{q^{\prime}},\eta_{2}(t_{q^{\prime}})\big)=\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}\mathfrak{t}_{i}+\alpha\mathfrak{n}_{\mathcal{E}^{z_{0}}_{0,\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}}

such that

|\mathfrak{t}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathfrak{t}_{d-1}\wedge A^{-1}\big(\nabla_{\xi}p_{1}(z_{q^{\prime}},\eta_{1}(t_{q^{\prime}}))-\nabla_{\xi}p_{2}(z_{q^{\prime}},\eta_{2}(t_{q^{\prime}}))|=|\alpha|.

This amounts to

\begin{split}|\langle\nabla_{\xi}p_{1}(z_{q},\xi_{1}(t_{q}))-\nabla_{\xi}p_{2}(z_{q},\xi_{2}(t_{q})),&A^{-1}(\nabla_{\xi}p_{1}(z_{q^{\prime}},\eta_{1}(t_{q^{\prime}}))-\nabla_{\xi}p_{2}(z_{q^{\prime}},\eta_{2}(t_{q^{\prime}})))\rangle|\\
&\gtrsim\nu^{2}.\end{split}

Let
\Delta_{v}=\nabla_{\xi}p_{1}(z_{q^{\prime}},\eta_{1}(t_{q^{\prime}}))-\nabla_{\xi}p_{2}(z_{q^{\prime}},\eta_{2}(t_{q^{\prime}}))
.
Finally, by Taylor expansion, the regularity,
\varepsilon
-smallness of the frequency support and
\varepsilon\ll\nu
, we find that the leading order term with an error of size
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon\nu)
is given by

(3.43)

|\langle\Delta_{v},(\partial_{\xi}^{2}p_{1}(z_{q^{\prime}},\xi_{1}(t_{q^{\prime}})))^{-1}\Delta_{v}\rangle|\gtrsim\nu^{2}

where the final estimate is a consequence of our hypothesis (
2.17
).

It remains to check for multiple intersections: First, suppose that the same bicharacteristic
T_{1}
intersects
T_{2}
at times
t_{1}
and
t_{2}\geq t_{1}+CR^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta}\nu^{-1}
. We compute the difference between the two as

\begin{split}(x_{T_{1}}-x_{T_{2}})(t)&=\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\partial_{\xi}p_{1}(x_{T_{1}}(s),s,\xi_{T_{1}}(s))-\partial_{\xi}p_{2}(x_{T_{2}}(s),s,\xi_{T_{2}}(s))ds\\
&=\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\partial_{\xi}p_{1}(x_{T_{1}}(t_{1}),t_{1},\xi_{T_{1}}(t_{1}))-\partial_{\xi}p_{2}(x_{T_{2}}(t_{1}),t_{1},\xi_{T_{2}}(t_{1}))ds+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon C_{2}).\end{split}

Consequently, we have
|x_{T_{1}}(t)-x_{T_{2}}(t)|\gtrsim|t-t_{1}|\nu\gtrsim R^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta}
, which proves the separation of the bicharacteristics after one intersection and contradicts the hypothesis that the same bicharacteristic intersects
T_{2}
at separated times.

Next, suppose we have two bicharacteristics
T_{1}
,
T_{1}^{\prime}
satisfying
x_{T_{1}}(t_{q})=x_{T^{\prime}_{1}}(t_{q})=x_{q}
and with frequencies
\xi_{T_{1}},\xi_{T_{1}^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{E}^{z_{q}}_{0,\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}
. Suppose that the first intersects
T_{2}
at time
t_{1}
, the other at time
t_{2}\geq t_{1}
with
|t_{2}-t_{1}|\geq R^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta}\nu^{-1}
. We have

x_{T_{1}}(t_{1})-x_{T_{1}^{\prime}}(t_{1})+x_{T_{2}}(t_{2})-x_{T_{2}}(t_{1})=x_{T_{1}^{\prime}}(t_{2})-x_{T_{1}^{\prime}}(t_{1}).

We can write equivalently

\begin{split}x_{T_{1}}(t_{1})-x_{T_{1}^{\prime}}(t_{1})&=(x_{T_{1}^{\prime}}(t_{2})-x_{T_{1}^{\prime}}(t_{1}))-(x_{T_{2}}(t_{2})-x_{T_{2}}(t_{1}))\\
&=(t_{2}-t_{1})(\nabla_{\xi}p_{1}(x_{T_{1}}(t_{*}),t_{*},\xi_{T_{1}}(t_{*}))-\nabla_{\xi}p_{2}(x_{T_{2}}(t_{*}),t_{*},\xi_{T_{2}}(t_{*}))).\end{split}

Let
\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathcal{E}^{z_{q}}_{0,\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}
which connects the two frequencies
\xi_{T_{1}},\xi_{T_{1}^{\prime}}
. We write the lefthand side of the preceding display as

\frac{\partial x_{t}}{\partial\eta}\frac{d\eta(\gamma(s^{*}))}{ds}=(t_{2}-t_{1})(\nabla_{\xi}p_{1}(x_{T_{1}}(t_{*}),t_{*},\xi_{T_{1}}(t_{*}))-\nabla_{\xi}p_{2}(x_{T_{2}}(t_{*}),t_{*},\xi_{T_{2}}(t_{*}))).

Clearly,
\mathfrak{t}=\frac{d\eta(\gamma(s^{*}))}{ds}
is an element of the tangent space
T_{\eta^{*}}\mathcal{E}^{z_{q}}_{0,\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}
at some frequency
\eta^{*}
in
\mathcal{E}^{z_{q}}_{0,\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}
. This frequency yields a point
x^{*}\in\Gamma_{q}(t)
. It follows a reprise of the above argument to rule out the multiple intersections.

We write again by the Hamiltonian flow

\begin{split}\frac{\partial x_{t}}{\partial\xi}&=\int_{t_{q}}^{t_{1}}\frac{\partial^{2}p}{(\partial\xi)^{2}}\frac{\partial\xi_{s}}{\partial\xi}+\frac{\partial^{2}p}{\partial x\partial\xi}\frac{\partial x_{s}}{\partial\xi}ds\\
&=(t_{1}-t_{q})(\partial^{2}_{\xi}p(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})(1+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon))+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon))=(t_{1}-t_{q})A^{*}.\end{split}

In particular, by our non-degeneracy assumption
\partial_{\xi}x_{t}
is invertible.

We can summarize the above as

(t_{1}-t_{q})/(t_{2}-t_{1})\mathfrak{t}=(A^{*})^{-1}(\nabla_{\xi}p_{1}(x_{T_{1}}(t_{*}),t_{*},\xi_{T_{1}}(t_{*}))-\nabla_{\xi}p_{2}(x_{T_{2}}(t_{*}),t_{*},\xi_{T_{2}}(t_{*}))).

Now we project to an unnormalized normal vector
\mathfrak{n}
at
\mathfrak{t}
which is given by

\mathfrak{n}=\nabla_{\xi}p_{1}(x^{*},t_{1},\eta_{1}^{*})-\nabla_{\xi}p_{2}(x^{*},t_{1},\eta_{2}^{*}),

and we find

0=\langle\mathfrak{n},(A^{*})^{-1}(\nabla_{\xi}p_{1}(x_{T_{1}}(t_{*}),t_{*},\xi_{T_{1}}(t_{*}))-\nabla_{\xi}p_{2}(x_{T_{2}}(t_{*}),t_{*},\xi_{T_{2}}(t_{*})))\rangle.

Let
\Delta_{v}=\nabla_{\xi}p_{1}(x_{T_{1}}(t_{*}),t_{*},\xi_{T_{1}}(t_{*}))-\nabla_{\xi}p_{2}(x_{T_{2}}(t_{*}),t_{*},\xi_{T_{2}}(t_{*})))
.
To conclude a contradiction, we carry out a Taylor expansion, which yields by size and regularity estimates on
p_{i}
:

0=\langle\Delta_{v},(\partial^{2}_{\xi}p_{1}(x_{T_{1}}(t_{*}),t_{*},\xi_{T_{1}}(t_{*})))^{-1}\Delta_{v}\rangle+\mathcal{O}(\nu\varepsilon)\gtrsim\nu^{2}.

This contradiction shows that there can be no multiple intersections of
\Gamma_{q}
with
T_{2}
.
∎

3.5.
Proof of Theorem
2.16

To conclude the proof of the theorem, we need remains to show the combinatorial estimate (
3.40
) for
1
-homogeneous phase functions. As in the proof of Theorem
2.13
, we proceed in two steps. In the first step, we prove the counterpart of Lemma
3.5
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
q^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{q}
such that there is
T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}:(q^{\prime},T_{1})\in\mathcal{Q}_{q}
. Let

\mathcal{Q}_{q}(q^{\prime})=\{T_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}(\mathcal{E}^{q}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}):(q^{\prime},T_{1})\in\mathcal{Q}_{q}\}.

Then it holds

|\mathcal{Q}_{q}(q^{\prime})|\lesssim R^{c\delta}.

Proof.

Again we shall see that
T_{1}
is essentially determined by
q
and
q^{\prime}
due to the bi-Lipschitz property of the flow. We observe that for
1
-homogeneous
p_{1}
the following is immediate:

Observation
(\star)
: Let
(x_{t},\xi^{\lambda}_{t})
be the bicharacteristic with initial data
(x_{0},\lambda\xi_{0})
. Then it holds
(x_{t},\xi^{\lambda}_{t})=(x_{0},\lambda\xi_{t})
.

In the argument we can only use the non-degeneracy in the angular direction of
p_{i}
. Indeed, for two frequencies
\xi_{1}
,
\xi_{2}
with very small angular separation:

\big|\frac{\xi_{1}}{|\xi_{1}|}-\frac{\xi_{2}}{|\xi_{2}|}\big|\lesssim R^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta}

it is easy to see that the tubes with the same starting point are comparable on a scale of
R^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta}
by the below.

We obtain for the bicharacteristic emanating from
x_{0}
with frequency
\xi_{0}
,
|\xi_{0}|=1
:

x(t)=x_{0}+t\frac{\partial p_{1}}{\partial\xi}(x_{0},0,\xi_{0})+t^{2}\zeta(t,x_{0},\xi_{0})

For the remainder of the Taylor expansion we have the following Lagrange representation:

\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)\big[\frac{\partial^{2}p_{1}}{\partial x\partial\xi}(x_{s},\xi_{s})\frac{\partial p_{1}}{\partial\xi}(x_{s},\xi_{s})-\frac{\partial^{2}p_{1}}{\partial\xi^{2}}(x_{s},\xi_{s})\frac{\partial p_{1}}{\partial x}(x_{s},\xi_{s})\big]ds=t^{2}\zeta(t,x_{0},\xi_{0})

with
\zeta
a continuous function and bounds only depending on
\varepsilon
and
C_{2}
.

We compare two tubes with
R^{1-\delta}\leq|t|\leq R
and frequencies
\xi_{1}
,
\xi_{2}
:

\begin{split}x_{1}(t)&=x_{0}+t\frac{\partial p_{1}}{\partial\xi}(x_{0},\xi_{1})+t^{2}\zeta(t,x_{0},\xi_{1}),\\
x_{2}(t)&=x_{0}+t\frac{\partial p_{1}}{\partial\xi}(x_{0},\xi_{2})+t^{2}\zeta(t,x_{0},\xi_{2}).\end{split}

By
(\star)
we can normalize
\|\xi_{i}\|=1
and retain the same curves
x_{i}(t)
.
It follows from the non-degeneracy in the angular directions

\big|\frac{\partial p_{1}}{\partial\xi}(x_{0},\xi_{1})-\frac{\partial p_{1}}{\partial\xi}(x_{0},\xi_{2})\big|=\big|\frac{\partial^{2}p_{1}}{(\partial\xi)^{2}}(x_{0},\xi_{*})\big(\frac{\xi_{1}}{|\xi_{1}|}-\frac{\xi_{2}}{|\xi|}\big)\big|\sim_{d_{1},d_{2}}\big|\frac{\xi_{1}}{|\xi_{1}|}-\frac{\xi_{2}}{|\xi|}\big|.

Secondly,

\big|\zeta(t,x_{0},\xi_{1})-\zeta(t,x_{0},\xi_{2})\big|\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon Ct^{2}}{R}\big|\frac{\xi_{1}}{|\xi_{1}|}-\frac{\xi_{2}}{|\xi|}\big|.

And it follows that the angular frequency is essentially uniquely determined by cubes
q
,
q^{\prime}
.
∎

We continue with the counterpart of Lemma
3.6
. Together, the next lemma and the previous one complete the proof of the desired bound (
3.40
).

Lemma 3.8
.

It holds

\big(\bigcup_{q^{\prime}:\mathcal{Q}_{q}(q^{\prime})\neq\emptyset}q^{\prime}\big)\cap T_{2}\subseteq T_{2}\big|_{I_{T}}

with
I_{T}
an interval of length
\nu^{-1}R^{\frac{1}{2}+C\delta}
.

Proof.

The argument follows along the above lines, but since
p
is homogeneous, we cannot use the non-degeneracy. So, like above, let

\Gamma_{q}(t)=\{(x(t),t):(x(t),\xi(t))\text{ solves }\eqref{eq:RescaledHamiltonianFlowAssumptions},\;x(t_{q})=x_{q},\;\xi(t_{q})\in\mathcal{E}^{z_{q}}_{0,\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}\}

describe the time-slice of the spatial projection of the bicharacteristic curves

(x(t),\xi(t))
with
x(t_{q})=x_{q}
and
\xi(t_{q})=\eta\in\mathcal{E}^{z_{q}}_{0,\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}
and let
\Gamma_{q}=\bigcup_{t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}}\Gamma_{q}(t)
.

Again suppose that
t_{q}=0
and
0=t_{q}<t_{q^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{I}_{T}=[\frac{R^{1-\delta}}{20},R]
.

Consider a tube
T_{2}\in\mathbb{T}_{2}
which intersects
\Gamma_{q}
at
(x_{q^{\prime}},t^{\prime})
. We show again the
\nu
-transversality of
T_{2}
with the tangent space of
\Gamma_{q}
. The perturbative arguments, i.e. checking the
R^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta}
-neighborhood of the energy shell and for multiple intersections can be done essentially like above, so this is omitted here.

Presently, we have
d-1
spatial directions obtained from varying
x_{\eta_{1}}
with variational frequencies in
T_{\eta_{1}}\mathcal{E}^{z_{q}}_{0,\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}
, the intersection frequency, and

V_{1}=(\partial_{\xi}p_{1}(x_{q^{\prime}},t^{\prime},\eta_{1}(t^{\prime})),1)
. We suppose by
(\star)
that
\|\eta_{1}\|=1
. Also, let
V_{2}=(\partial_{\xi}p_{2}(x_{q^{\prime}},t^{\prime},\eta_{2}(t^{\prime})),1)
denote the tangent direction of
T_{2}
at the intersection point. Recall the variation formula from above:

\frac{\partial x_{t}}{\partial\xi}=\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}}\frac{\partial^{2}p}{\partial\xi\partial x}\frac{\partial x_{t}}{\partial\xi}ds+\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}}\frac{\partial^{2}p}{\partial\xi^{2}}\frac{\partial\xi_{t}}{\partial\xi}ds.

By the mean-value theorem we let

\frac{\partial x_{t}}{\partial\xi}=t^{\prime}\big(\frac{\partial^{2}p_{1}}{\partial x\partial\xi}(x_{*},\xi_{*})\frac{\partial x_{t}}{\partial\xi}+\frac{\partial^{2}p_{1}}{(\partial\xi)^{2}}(x_{*},\xi_{*})\frac{\partial\xi_{t_{*}}}{\partial\xi}\big)=t^{\prime}A^{*}.

It will suffice to check the transversality

(3.44)

\big|\frac{\partial^{2}p_{1}}{(\partial\xi)^{2}}(x_{*},\xi_{*})\mathfrak{t}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\frac{\partial^{2}p_{1}}{(\partial\xi)^{2}}(x_{*},\xi_{*})\mathfrak{t}_{d-1}\wedge V_{1}\wedge V_{2}\big|\gtrsim\nu

where
\mathfrak{t}_{i}
denote the normalized tangent vectors spanning
T_{\eta_{1}}\mathcal{E}_{0,\xi_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}^{z_{q}}
.

This reduction follows from estimating

\big|\frac{\partial^{2}p_{1}}{\partial x\partial\xi}(x_{*},\xi_{*})\frac{\partial x_{t}}{\partial\xi}\big|\lesssim\varepsilon\text{ and }\big|\frac{\partial\xi_{t_{*}}}{\partial\xi}-I_{d}\big|\lesssim\varepsilon.

By elimination, another Taylor expansion and using the confinement of the frequencies, it suffices to check that

\big|\frac{\partial^{2}p_{1}}{(\partial\xi)^{2}}(x_{q}^{\prime},\xi_{1}^{*})\mathfrak{t}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\frac{\partial^{2}p_{1}}{(\partial\xi)^{2}}(x_{q}^{\prime},\xi_{1}^{*})\mathfrak{t}_{d-1}\wedge V_{1}-V_{2}\big|\gtrsim\nu.

To prove the above estimate, we can argue equivalently for
A_{\xi_{1}^{*}}=\frac{\partial^{2}p_{1}}{(\partial\xi)^{2}}(x_{q}^{\prime},\xi_{1}^{*})
:

\text{dist}(V_{1}-V_{2},\text{R}(A_{\xi_{1}^{*}}))\gtrsim\nu.

Now we use self-adjointness and
\text{R}(A_{\xi_{1}^{*}})=\ker(A_{\xi_{1}^{*}})^{\perp}=\langle\xi_{1}^{*}\rangle^{\perp}
by
1
-homogeneity.

Consequently, this is equivalent to

|\langle\xi_{1}^{*},\nabla_{\xi}p_{1}(x_{q^{\prime}},t^{\prime},\eta_{1}(t^{\prime}))-\nabla_{\xi}p_{2}(x_{q^{\prime}},t^{\prime},\eta_{2}(t^{\prime}))\rangle|\gtrsim\nu,

which is true by our hypothesis (
2.20
).
∎

4.
Wave packet decompositions for rough flows

In this section we construct wave packet decompositions to

(4.45)

(D_{t}-a^{w}(x,t,D))u=f,\quad u(0)=u_{0}

for
a\in C^{\infty}_{t,x}C_{\xi}^{\infty}
and phase-space localized
u_{0}
. To comprehend the regularity assumptions on
a
, recall that in the outset we consider a dyadic frequency localization
N\gg 1
with a corresponding paradifferential decomposition. For real
a
, we find that
a^{w}
is self-adjoint, and the above PDE generates an isometric evolution operator
S(t,s)
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
The key examples are
1
-homogeneous symbols, corresponding to variable-coefficient wave equations, and symbols quadratic in
\xi
(
2
-homogeneous), corresponding to variable-coefficient Schrödinger equations. We denote the homogeneity by
\mathfrak{h}
. For
\mathfrak{h}=1
, we have the important example

(4.46)

a(x,t,\xi)=(g^{ij}_{\leq N^{\frac{1}{2}}}(x,t)\xi_{i}\xi_{j})^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad|\xi|\sim N.

In this case we consider
|t|\leq 1
and we can localize to
|x|\lesssim 1
.

For
\mathfrak{h}=2
, we have

(4.47)

a(x,t,\xi)=g^{ij}_{\leq N,\leq N^{\frac{1}{2}}}(x,t)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}.

Here we consider times
|t|\lesssim N^{-1}
such that we can still localize spatially
|x|\lesssim 1
and due to parabolic scaling, we can consider a more relaxed paradifferential decomposition with respect to time. We shall see that it suffices to consider
C^{1}
-regularity in time.

Our phase space transform will be formulated for the unit frequency evolution on a large time scale
R
. The transition between the examples above and this normalized setting is carried out by scaling. Precisely, with
R=N
, in the case wave case
\mathfrak{h}=1
we rescale both space and time by
R
, while in the Schrödinger case we use the anisotropic rescaling by
(R^{2},R)
for time respectively space.
The regularity assumptions on the Hamiltonian flow are formulated as follows:

Assumption 4.1
.

Let
R\gg 1
. Let
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{d}
which satisfies Assumption
2.3
with
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
a\in C^{\infty}_{z,\xi}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We require that the flow-out of
\mathcal{Y}
under the Hamiltonian flow
a
for
t\in[-10R,10R]
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\dot{x}^{t}&=\frac{\partial a}{\partial\xi},\\
\dot{\xi}^{t}&=-\frac{\partial a}{\partial x}\end{array}\right.

is contained in
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
. We require the following regularity estimates:

(4.48)

|\partial_{z}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}a(x,t;\xi)|\lesssim_{\alpha,\beta}\begin{cases}R^{-|\alpha|},&\quad 0\leq|\alpha|\leq 2,\\
R^{-\frac{(|\alpha|-2)_{+}}{2}-2},&\quad|\alpha|\geq 2.\end{cases}

We obtain wave packets from applying the modified FBI transform with parameter
R
on the time-scale
|t|\leq R
. The wave packets are of size
\Delta x\sim R^{\frac{1}{2}}
,
\Delta\xi\sim R^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.
Note that it means for a general variable-coefficient Schrödinger evolution for frequencies
N\gg 1
we only find a parametrix on times
\lesssim N^{-1}
. This is unsurprising as for larger times, by infinite speed of propagation, the global behavior of the Hamiltonian flow would have to play a role. We shall see that wave packets constructed this way satisfy the axioms from Assumption
2.5
and comply with the evolution generated by the PDE as in Assumption
2.8
.

Our objective in this section will be to prove
the result in Theorem
1.2
on wave packet decompositions, which is stated in the introduction but we recall it here for convenience:

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
R\gg 1
, and
\nu\in[R^{-\frac{1}{2}+\delta_{0}},1]
. Suppose that
a
is a Hamiltonian, which satisfies Assumption
4.1
, with the set
\mathcal{Y}
satisfying Assumption
2.3
. Let
r\in[\nu^{-2-\delta_{0}},R]
and
\mathcal{Y}_{r},\,\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}
be defined like in Definition
2.4
.
There is a pseudo-differential operator
\chi_{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}}(x,D):L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that for solutions to

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}(D_{t}-a^{w})u&=0,\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1},\\
u(0)&=\chi_{\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}}u_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\end{array}\right.

we have the decomposition in
L^{2}
for
t\in[-r,r]
with a rapidly decaying error term

u=\sum_{(x_{0},\xi_{0})\in\Lambda_{r}\cap\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}}\alpha_{(x_{0},\xi_{0})}\phi_{(x_{0},\xi_{0})}(t)+g(t)

with

\|g(t)\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim\nu^{d\big(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}\big)}\text{RapDec}(r\nu^{2})\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}

for
2\leq p\leq\infty
and
(\phi_{(x_{0},\xi_{0})})_{(x_{0},\xi_{0})\in\Lambda_{r}\cap\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{r}}
are normalized wave packets satisfying Assumption
2.5
.

4.1.
Decomposition into coherent states

The parametrix for rough coefficients will be based on the modified FBI transform:

(4.49)

\begin{split}T_{R}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})&\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d}),\\
T_{R}f(x,\xi)&=C_{R}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\xi(x-y)}e^{-\frac{\rho}{2}(x-y)^{2}}f(y)dy,\quad(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\end{split}

for functions
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
\rho=R^{-1}
. The normalizing constant is given by
C_{R}=R^{-\frac{d}{4}}2^{-\frac{d}{2}}\pi^{-\frac{d}{4}}
.
T_{R}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
is an isometry and maps onto the closed subspace of analytic functions identifying
\mathbb{R}^{2d}\tilde{=}\mathbb{C}^{d}
, letting
z=x-i\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
One inversion formula is provided by the adjoint:

f(y)=C_{R}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}e^{-\frac{\rho}{2}(x-y)^{2}}e^{-i\xi(x-y)}F(x,\xi)dxd\xi.

We remark that after applying
T_{R}
the frequencies are essentially normalized.
The FBI transform decomposes a function into coherent states,
which exhaust the uncertainty relation, and are given as follows:

(4.50)

\phi_{x,\xi}(y)=e^{i\xi(x-y)}e^{-\frac{\rho}{2}(x-y)^{2}}.

These are localized in space on a scale
R^{\frac{1}{2}}
around
x
and on the frequency side on a scale of
R^{-\frac{1}{2}}
around
\xi
. We can write the modified FBI transform as

T_{R}f(x,\xi)=C_{R}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi_{x,\xi}(y)f(y)dy.

The FBI transform conjugates the evolution of (
4.45
) with well-controlled error terms to the Hamiltonian flow governed by
a
in phase space.
By these means, in a series of papers, the second author
[
36
,
38
,
39
]
proved Strichartz estimates for wave equations with
C^{1,1}
-coefficients with the same derivative loss like on Euclidean space (see also Tataru
[
40
]
and Koch–Tataru
[
22
]
for parametrices for symbols in a restricted
S^{0}_{0,0}
-class).
We remark that for
C^{1,1}
-coefficients, which represents the natural threshold for classical well-posedness of the Hamiltonian flow, after the paradifferential decomposition explained in the beginning of the section, Assumption
4.1
is satisfied.

We denote the solutions to the Hamiltonian flow with initial data
(x^{0},\xi^{0})=(x,\xi)
by
x^{t}(x,\xi)
,
\xi^{t}(x,\xi)
and define the phase shift
\psi
as solution to the equation

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{d}{dt}\psi(x^{t},t,\xi^{t})&=-a(x^{t},t,\xi^{t})+\xi^{t}\frac{\partial a}{\partial\xi},\quad(x,t,\xi)\in\overline{\mathcal{Y}}\\
\psi(x,0,\xi)&=0.\end{array}\right.

We note that for
\mathfrak{h}\in\{1,2\}
we have

\psi(t,x^{t},\xi^{t})=(\mathfrak{h}-1)\int_{0}^{t}a(s,x^{s},\xi^{s})ds.

An explicit formula for the solution to the homogeneous equation (
4.45
) with
f=0
was provided by the second author in
[
40
]
, see also the later paper
[
22
]
. Here we have the following:

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
R\gg 1
. The kernel
K
of the fundamental solution
(D_{t}-a^{w})
can be represented in the form

(4.51)

\begin{split}K(t,y,s,\tilde{y})&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}e^{-\frac{\rho}{2}(\tilde{y}-x^{s})^{2}}e^{-i\xi^{s}(\tilde{y}-x^{s})}e^{i(\psi(t,x,\xi)-\psi(s,x,\xi))}e^{i\xi^{t}\cdot(y-x^{t})}\\
&\quad\quad\times G(t,s,x,\xi,y)dxd\xi,\end{split}

where the function
G
satisfies

\|(R^{-\frac{1}{2}}(x^{t}-y))^{\gamma}\partial_{t}^{\sigma}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{y}^{\nu}G(t,s,x,\xi,y)\|_{L_{y}^{2}}\lesssim c_{\nu,\alpha,\beta,\gamma}R^{-\frac{|\alpha|-|\beta|+|\nu|+|\sigma|}{2}}C_{R}^{2}\|\phi_{x,\xi}\|_{2}

for
|t-s|\lesssim R
.

In the case of normalized frequencies on the unit time interval for symbols in a restricted
S^{0}
-class, this is
[
40
, Theorem 5]
. We carry out a detailed analysis to carefully track the dependence of the constants on frequency and time.

Beginning of the Proof of Proposition
4.3
.
The argument follows
[
22
, Proposition 4.3]
. But here we use the rescaled phase space transform.

We let
s=0
without loss of generality. For
u_{0}\in L^{2}
we use the modified FBI transform to write
u_{0}
as superposition into coherent states:

(4.52)

u(t,y)=S(t,0)T_{R}^{*}T_{R}u_{0}=C_{R}\int S(t,0)\phi_{x,\xi}(y)(T_{R}u_{0})(x,\xi)dxd\xi

with coherent states given by (
4.50
). The function
G
is correspondingly defined by

G(t,x,\xi,y)=C^{2}_{R}e^{-i\xi^{t}(y-x^{t})}e^{-i\psi(t,x,\xi)}(S(t,0)\phi_{x,\xi})(y).

It remains to verify the bounds on
G
, which rely on bounds for the regularity of the Hamiltonian flow. This will be carried out in Section
4.2
.

With (
4.52
) at hand, we proceed to the next step:

Construction of wave packets:
We use a smooth partition in phase space. Let
\psi_{x_{0},\xi_{0}}(x,\xi)
denote a smooth bump function with

\text{supp}(\psi_{x_{0},\xi_{0}})\subseteq B(x_{0},3R^{\frac{1}{2}})\times B(\xi_{0},3R^{-\frac{1}{2}}).

Let
\Lambda_{R}=R^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times R^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. We require that for any
(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
:

\sum_{(x_{0},\xi_{0})\in\Lambda_{R}}\psi_{x_{0},\xi_{0}}(x,\xi)\equiv 1.

We obtain a decomposition of the solution:

\begin{split}u&=S(t,0)T_{R}^{*}T_{R}u_{0}=C_{R}S(t,0)T_{R}^{*}\sum_{(x_{0},\xi_{0})\in\Lambda_{R}}\psi_{x_{0},\xi_{0}}(x,\xi)(T_{R}u_{0})\\
&=C_{R}\sum_{(x_{0},\xi_{0})\in\Lambda_{R}}\int(S(t,0)\phi_{x,\xi})\psi_{x_{0},\xi_{0}}(x,\xi)(T_{R}u_{0})(x,\xi),\end{split}

and we obtain a decomposition of the kernel:

\begin{split}&\quad K(t,y,s,\tilde{y})\\
&=\sum_{(x_{0},\xi_{0})\in\Lambda_{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}e^{-\frac{\rho}{2}(\tilde{y}-x^{s})^{2}}e^{-i\xi^{s}(\tilde{y}-x^{s})}e^{i\xi^{t}(y-x^{t})}e^{i(\psi(t,x,\xi)-\psi(s,x,\xi))}\\
&\quad\quad\times\psi_{x_{0},\xi_{0}}(x,\xi)G(t,s,x,\xi,y)dxd\xi=\sum_{(x_{0},\xi_{0})\in\Lambda_{R}}K_{x_{0},\xi_{0}}(t,y,s,\tilde{y}).\end{split}

What we expect is that the kernel localized to
(x_{0},\xi_{0})
acting on the initial data,

u_{x_{0},\xi_{0}}(t,y)=\int K_{x_{0},\xi_{0}}(t,y,0,\tilde{y})u_{0}(\tilde{y})d\tilde{y},

has frequency support essentially in an
R^{-\frac{1}{2}}
-ball around
\xi_{0}^{t}
and spatial support in an
R^{\frac{1}{2}}
-ball around
x_{0}^{t}
. Indeed, the above formula, together with regularity of the Hamiltonian flow and
G
, already indicates that the wave packets have the desired shape: they can be regarded as curved tubes centered at bicharacteristics with radius of size
R^{\frac{1}{2}}
; see Lemma
2.2
.

Remark 4.4
.

We note that the phase space localization by
\psi_{x_{0},\xi_{0}}
accomplishes precisely the outset of Lemma
2.2
with
\delta=0
.

The bounds for
G
are proved in the next section. Once we have established the regularity of
G
claimed in Proposition
4.3
, we conclude the localization properties of the wave packets in Section
4.3
. At the end of Section
4.3
we introduce the weight in phase space and finish the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

4.2.
The regularity of the Hamiltonian flow

This section is devoted to the analysis of the Hamiltonian flow. We shall see that on the frequency-dependent time
|t|\lesssim R^{\frac{1}{2}}
the Hamiltonian flow is smooth. Naturally, the coherent states
\phi_{x,\xi}
retain their shape and can be regarded as
\phi_{x_{t},\xi_{t}}
. For
|t|\gg R^{\frac{1}{2}}
, the Hamiltonian flow ceases to be smooth and blows up exponentially: but we can take advantage of the localization of the Gaussians and argue that it persists up to times
|t|\lesssim R
.

The main tool to close the bootstrap assumption on the localization of
S(t,0)\phi_{x,\xi}
are energy estimates. With the above in mind, we would like to regard
S(t,0)\phi_{x,\xi}\approx e^{i\xi^{t}\cdot(x^{t}-y)}e^{-\frac{\rho}{2}(x^{t}-y)^{2}}
such that

G(t,x,\xi,y)\approx C_{R}^{2}e^{-\frac{\rho}{2}(x^{t}-y)^{2}}.

This explains the estimates in Proposition
4.3
.

For ease of presentation, we state the regularity of the Hamiltonian flow in a series of lemmas. We begin with the regularity in frequency variables, which up to a rescaling is
[
38
, Lemma 9]
. We revisit the proof because the arguments will be important to obtain the joint regularity in position and frequency variables.

To be precise, we need to prove bounds on the Hamiltonian flow associated with
a
satisfying Assumption
4.1
.

The regularity of the Hamiltonian flow is easier to analyze and state for normalized time as various Grønwall arguments are involved. This leads us to analyze Hamiltonians on the unit time interval which satisfy the regularity assumption:

(4.53)

|\partial_{x,t}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}a(x,t,\xi)|\lesssim_{\alpha,\beta}R^{\frac{(|\alpha|-2)_{+}}{2}}.

In the following we analyze in detail the Hamiltonian flow associated with
a
satisfying (
4.53
) on the unit time interval
|t|\lesssim 1
:

(4.54)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\dot{x}^{t}&=\partial_{\xi}a(x^{t},t,\xi^{t}),\\
\dot{\xi}^{t}&=-\partial_{x}a(x^{t},t,\xi^{t}).\end{array}\right.

When the explicit time-dependence plays no role in the following, it will be suppressed to lighten the notation.

Lemma 4.5

(Regularity in frequency variables)
.

For
|\beta|\geq 1
, the Hamiltonian flow (
4.54
) satisfies for
|t|\leq 2
the following regularity estimates:

(4.55)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}|\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}x^{t}|&\lesssim_{\beta}|t|(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta|-1},\\
|\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\xi^{t}|&\lesssim_{\beta}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta|-1}.\end{array}\right.

Proof.

For
|\beta|=1
, using the differentiability of the flow, we find

(4.56)

\frac{d}{dt}\begin{pmatrix}\partial x^{t}/\partial\xi\\
\partial\xi^{t}/\partial\xi\end{pmatrix}=\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{x\xi}^{2}a&\partial^{2}_{\xi\xi}a\\
-\partial_{xx}^{2}a&-\partial_{x\xi}^{2}a\end{pmatrix}}_{A(t)}\begin{pmatrix}\partial x^{t}/\partial\xi\\
\partial\xi^{t}/\partial\xi\end{pmatrix}.

A(t)
is clearly bounded by
a\in C^{1,1}
uniformly. Moreover,
\partial x^{0}/\partial\xi=0
,
\partial\xi^{0}/\partial\xi=I_{d}
. This gives the bounds for
|\beta|=1
and
|t|\lesssim 1
.

For the induction step
|\beta|>1
we find by taking
|\beta|-1
derivatives of (
4.56
):

\frac{d}{dt}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}x^{t}\\
\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\xi^{t}\end{pmatrix}=A(t)\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}x^{t}\\
\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\xi^{t}\end{pmatrix}+\eta.

The inhomogeneity consists of lower order terms

\eta=\sum_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\zeta}\mu_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\zeta}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{1}}(\partial^{2}_{\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}}a)(x^{t},\xi^{t})\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{2}}\partial_{\xi}\zeta^{t},

with
\kappa_{i},\zeta\in\{x;\xi\}
,
|\beta|-1=|\beta_{1}|+|\beta_{2}|
,
|\beta_{2}|<|\beta|-1
.
\mu_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\zeta}\in\mathbb{N}
denote coefficients. The first condition is a consequence of the Leibniz’s rule, and the second condition reflects that in the inhomogeneity we are collecting the terms for which not all derivatives fall on the second term in (
4.56
).

Furthermore, computing
\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{1}}\partial^{2}_{\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}}a
by the chain rule, we find in one case expressions of the form

(4.57)

\begin{split}&\quad\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{1}}\partial^{2}_{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}}a\\
&=\sum_{\beta_{11},\beta_{12}}\mu_{\beta_{11},\beta_{12}}\big[(\partial^{\beta_{11}}_{x}\partial^{\beta_{12}}_{\xi}\partial^{2}_{xx}a)(x^{t},\xi^{t})\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{11}|}(\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{11}^{k}}x^{t})\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{12}|}(\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{12}^{k}}\xi^{t})\big]\;\cdot\;(\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{2}}\partial_{\xi}x^{t}),\end{split}

where

•

1\leq|\beta_{11}|+|\beta_{12}|\leq|\beta_{1}|\leq|\beta_{1}|+|\beta_{2}|=|\beta|-1
,
|\beta_{2}|<|\beta|-1
,

•

1\leq|\beta_{11}^{k_{1}}|,|\beta_{12}^{k_{2}}|\leq|\beta|-1
,

•

\sum_{k_{1}}|\beta_{11}^{k_{1}}|+\sum_{k_{2}}|\beta_{12}^{k_{2}}|=|\beta_{1}|
.

There are more terms, involving different second order derivatives of
a
in the first factor, but these are better behaved since
a
is smooth in
\xi
. On the other hand, the second factor might read
\partial_{\xi}\xi^{t}
, for which we have inferior estimates than for
\partial_{\xi}x^{t}
. We estimate the other terms in (
4.58
).

We have the bound (
4.53
). Plugging in the induction assumption and integrating we finish the proof of (
4.55
):

\begin{split}&\quad\int_{0}^{t}\big|\eqref{eq:InhomogeneityI}\big|ds\\
&\lesssim|t|\cdot R^{\frac{|\beta_{11}|}{2}}\big(\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{11}|}|t|(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta_{11}^{k}|-1}\big)\,\cdot\,\big(\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{12}|}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta_{12}^{k}|-1}\big)\\
&\quad\times\big(|t|(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta_{2}|}\big)\\
&\lesssim|t|R^{\frac{|\beta_{11}|}{2}}|t|^{|\beta_{11}|}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{\sum_{k}|\beta_{11}^{k}|-|\beta_{11}|}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{\sum_{k}|\beta_{12}^{k}|-|\beta_{12}|}\\
&\lesssim|t|^{2}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta|-1-|\beta_{12}|},\end{split}

which in the case
|\beta_{12}|=0
is trivially estimated by the above.

A second term we need to estimate is given by

(4.58)

\big[(\partial^{\beta_{11}}_{x}\partial^{\beta_{12}}_{\xi}\partial^{2}_{x\xi}a)(x^{t},\xi^{t})\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{11}|}(\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{11}^{k}}x^{t})\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{12}|}(\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{12}^{k}}\xi^{t})\big]\;\cdot\;(\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{2}}\partial_{\xi}\xi^{t}),

for which we find the estimate for
|\beta_{2}|>0
:

\begin{split}&\quad\int_{0}^{t}\big|\eqref{eq:InhomogeneityII}\big|ds\\
&\lesssim|t|R^{\frac{(|\beta_{11}|-1)_{+}}{2}}\big(\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{11}|}|t|(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta_{11}^{k}|-1}\big)\,\cdot\,\big(\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{12}|}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta_{12}^{k}|-1}\big)\\
&\quad\times\big((1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta_{2}|}\big)\\
&\lesssim|t|R^{\frac{(|\beta_{11}|-1)_{+}}{2}}|t|^{|\beta_{11}|}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{\sum_{k}|\beta_{11}^{k}|+\sum_{k}|\beta_{12}^{k}|-|\beta_{11}|-|\beta_{12}|+|\beta_{2}|}\\
&\lesssim|t|(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta_{1}|}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta|-1-|\beta_{1}|-|\beta_{2}|}\\
&\lesssim|t|(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta|-1}.\end{split}

For
\beta_{2}=0
we find

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{t}\big|\eqref{eq:InhomogeneityII}\big|ds&\lesssim|t|R^{\frac{(|\beta_{11}|-1)_{+}}{2}}\big(\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{11}|}|t|(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta_{11}^{k}|-1}\big)\,\cdot\,\big(\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{12}|}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta_{12}^{k}|-1}\big)\\
&\lesssim|t|R^{\frac{(|\beta_{11}|-1)_{+}}{2}}|t|^{|\beta_{11}|}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{\sum_{k}|\beta_{11}^{k}|+\sum_{k}|\beta_{12}^{k}|-|\beta_{11}|-|\beta_{12}|}\\
&\lesssim|t|(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta|-1}.\end{split}

The proof is complete.
∎

Lemma 4.6

(Regularity of the Hamiltonian flow in position variables)
.

The Hamiltonian flow (
4.54
) satisfies the following estimates:

(4.59)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}|\partial_{x}x^{t}|&\lesssim 1,\\
|\partial_{x}\xi^{t}|&\lesssim|t|,\end{array}\right.

and for
|\alpha|\geq 2
, we find

(4.60)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}x^{t}|&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\alpha|-1}{2}}|t|,\\
|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\xi^{t}|&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\alpha|-1}{2}}|t|.\end{array}\right.

Proof.

The argument is similar to the preceding proof. For
|\alpha|=1
we find

(4.61)

\frac{d}{dt}\begin{pmatrix}\partial x^{t}/\partial x\\
\partial\xi^{t}/\partial x\end{pmatrix}=\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{x\xi}^{2}a&\partial^{2}_{\xi\xi}a\\
-\partial_{xx}^{2}a&-\partial^{2}_{x\xi}a\end{pmatrix}}_{B(t)}\begin{pmatrix}\partial x^{t}/\partial x\\
\partial\xi^{t}/\partial x\end{pmatrix}

with initial data

\begin{pmatrix}\partial x^{0}/\partial x\\
\partial\xi^{0}/\partial x\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{d}\\
0\end{pmatrix}

and bounded
B(t)
. This yields (
4.59
).

We turn to the induction step
|\alpha|>1
, for which by taking
|\alpha|-1
derivatives of (
4.61
) we obtain

\frac{d}{dt}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}x^{t}\\
\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\xi^{t}\end{pmatrix}=B(t)\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}x^{t}\\
\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\xi^{t}\end{pmatrix}+\eta

with the inhomogeneity consisting of lower order terms. In the “worst” case we have terms of the form

\begin{split}&\quad\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}(\partial^{2}_{xx}a(x^{t},\xi^{t}))\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}(\partial x^{t}/\partial x)\\
&=\sum_{\alpha_{11},\alpha_{12}}(\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{11}}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha_{12}}\partial^{2}_{xx}a)(x^{t},\xi^{t})\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{11}|}(\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{11}^{k}}x^{t})\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{12}|}(\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{12}^{k}}\xi^{t})\cdot(\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\partial x^{t}/\partial x).\end{split}

The reason for the other terms being better behaved is that for the second derivative
\partial^{2}_{xx}a
any further derivative in
x
gives rise to factors of
R^{\frac{1}{2}}
, which makes the above term the worst case scenario. The conditions on the indices are given by

\sum_{k}|\alpha_{11}^{k}|+\sum_{k}|\alpha_{12}^{k}|=|\alpha_{1}|,\quad 1\leq|\alpha_{1i}^{k}|\leq|\alpha_{1}|\leq|\alpha|-1,\quad|\alpha_{1}|+|\alpha_{2}|=|\alpha|-1.

The induction assumption is applicable and gives

\begin{split}|\cdot|_{\alpha}&\lesssim|\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{11}}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha_{12}}\partial^{2}_{xx}a|\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{11}|}|\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{11}^{k}}x^{t}|\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{12}^{k}|}|\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{12}^{k}}\xi^{t}||\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\partial_{x}x^{t}|\\
&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\alpha_{11}|}{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{11}|}R^{\frac{|\alpha_{11}^{k}|-1}{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{12}|}(R^{\frac{|\alpha_{12}^{k}|-1}{2}}|t|)\cdot R^{\frac{|\alpha_{2}|}{2}}\\
&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\alpha_{1}|+|\alpha_{2}|}{2}}.\end{split}

Integration in
t
shows the claim.
∎

Next, we turn to the regularity of the Hamiltonian flow in position and frequency. We start with the following:

Lemma 4.7
.

The Hamiltonian flow (
4.54
) satisfies the following estimates for
|\beta|\geq 1
:

(4.62)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}|\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{x}x^{t}|&\lesssim|t|(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta|},\\
|\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{x}\xi^{t}|&\lesssim|t|(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta|}.\end{array}\right.

Proof.

The proof is again carried out by induction on
|\beta|
. Here the base case is the one for mixed derivatives of first order:

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dt}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\xi_{i}}(\partial x^{t}/\partial x)\\
\partial_{\xi_{i}}(\partial x^{t}/\partial x)\end{pmatrix}&=\partial_{\xi_{i}}\frac{d}{dt}\big[\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{x}x^{t}\\
\partial_{x}\xi^{t}\end{pmatrix}\big]=\partial_{\xi_{i}}(B(t)\begin{pmatrix}\partial x^{t}/\partial x\\
\partial\xi^{t}/\partial x\end{pmatrix})\\
&=(\partial_{\xi_{i}}B)\begin{pmatrix}\partial x^{t}/\partial x\\
\partial\xi^{t}/\partial x\end{pmatrix}+B(t)\begin{pmatrix}\partial^{2}x^{t}/(\partial\xi_{i}\partial x)\\
\partial^{2}\xi^{t}/(\partial\xi_{i}\partial x)\end{pmatrix}.\end{split}

We compute the coefficients of

\partial_{\xi_{i}}B=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial^{3}a}{\partial x\partial\xi\partial x}\partial_{\xi_{i}}x+\frac{\partial^{3}a}{\partial x(\partial\xi)^{2}}\partial_{\xi_{i}}\xi&\frac{\partial^{3}a}{(\partial\xi)^{2}\partial x}\partial_{\xi_{i}}x+\frac{\partial^{3}a}{(\partial\xi)^{3}}\partial_{\xi_{i}}\xi\\
-\frac{\partial^{3}a}{(\partial x)^{3}}\partial_{\xi_{i}}x-\frac{\partial^{3}a}{(\partial x)^{2}\partial\xi}\partial_{\xi_{i}}\xi&-\frac{\partial^{3}a}{\partial x\partial\xi\partial x}\partial_{\xi_{i}}x-\frac{\partial^{3}a}{\partial x(\partial\xi)^{2}}\partial_{\xi_{i}}\xi\end{pmatrix}.

Notably,
|\partial^{3}a/(\partial x)^{3}|\lesssim R^{\frac{1}{2}}
. The other derivatives are uniformly bounded. Since
(\partial_{\xi_{i}}B)_{21}
acts on
\partial_{x}x^{t}
, which is bounded by
1
, we obtain after integration

\int_{0}^{t}\big|(\partial_{\xi_{i}}B)\begin{pmatrix}\partial x^{t}/\partial x\\
\partial\xi^{t}/\partial x\end{pmatrix}\big|ds\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|s|)ds\lesssim|t|(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|).

This proves (
4.62
) for
|\beta|=1
because of vanishing initial data.

Next, we prove (
4.62
) for higher values of
|\beta|
by induction. To this end, we apply
\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}
to (
4.61
) which leads us to

\frac{d}{dt}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{x}x^{t}\\
\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{x}\xi^{t}\end{pmatrix}=B(t)\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{x}x^{t}\\
\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{x}\xi^{t}\end{pmatrix}+\eta

with inhomogeneity
\eta
also consists of terms of the form

\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{1}}(\partial^{2}_{xx}a)(x^{t},\xi^{t})\cdot\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{2}}\partial_{x}x^{t},\quad|\beta_{1}|+|\beta_{2}|=|\beta|,\quad|\beta_{2}|\leq|\beta|-1.

These terms again give rise to the worst case because we cannot afford anymore
x
-derivatives on
\partial^{2}_{xx}a
without losing factors of
R^{\frac{1}{2}}
. The inhomogeneous terms involving derivatives
\partial^{2}_{x\xi}a
or
\partial^{2}_{\xi\xi}a
are better behaved.

We expand the first factor by the chain rule like above:

(4.63)

=\big(\sum_{\beta_{11},\beta_{12}}\big[(\partial_{x}^{\beta_{11}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{12}}\partial^{2}_{xx}a)(x^{t},\xi^{t})\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{11}^{k}|}\big(\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{11}^{k}}x^{t}\big)\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{12}^{k}|}\big(\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{12}^{k}}\xi^{t}\big)\big]\big)\;\cdot\;\big(\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{2}}\partial_{x}x^{t}\big)

with indices satisfying

(1)

|\beta_{1}|+|\beta_{2}|=|\beta|
,
|\beta_{2}|\leq|\beta|-1
,

(2)

\sum_{k=1}^{|\beta_{11}|}|\beta_{11}^{k}|+\sum_{k=1}^{|\beta_{12}^{k}|}|\beta_{12}^{k}|=|\beta_{1}|,\quad|\beta_{1i}^{k}|\geq 1
.

For
|\beta_{2}|=0
we find the following bound for (
4.63
) for fixed
\beta
-indices:

\begin{split}|\cdot|_{\beta}&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\beta_{11}|}{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{11}|}|t|(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta_{11}^{k}|-1}\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{12}|}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta_{12}^{k}|-1}\\
&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\beta_{11}|}{2}}|t|^{|\beta_{11}|}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta_{1}|-|\beta_{11}|-|\beta_{12}|}\\
&\lesssim(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta_{1}|-|\beta_{12}|},\end{split}

which upon integration gives the claim.

For
|\beta_{2}|\geq 1
we obtain

\begin{split}&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\beta_{11}|}{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{11}|}|t|(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta_{11}^{k}|-1}\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{12}|}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta_{12}^{k}|-1}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta_{2}|}|t|^{|\beta_{2}|}\\
&\lesssim(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta|-|\beta_{12}|}|t|^{|\beta_{2}|},\end{split}

which is better than the first bound. The proof is complete.
∎

Finally, we formulate the estimates for the joint regularity:

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
|\alpha|\geq 1
and
|\beta|\geq 1
. The Hamiltonian flow (
4.54
) satisfies the following estimates:

(4.64)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}x^{t}|&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\alpha|-1}{2}}|t|(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta|},\\
|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\xi^{t}|&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\alpha|-1}{2}}|t|(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta|}.\end{array}\right.

Proof.

We prove the claim by induction on
|\alpha|+|\beta|
.
The base case
|\alpha|=1
and
|\beta|=1
has been treated above. The starting point is (
4.61
) to which we apply
\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}
. We arrive at

\frac{d}{dt}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{x}x^{t}\\
\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{x}\xi^{t}\end{pmatrix}=B(t)\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{x}x^{t}\\
\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{x}\xi^{t}\end{pmatrix}+\eta.

The “worst” terms in
\eta
stem from
\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}(\partial^{2}_{xx}a\cdot\partial_{x}x^{t})
, where not all derivatives
\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}
hit
\partial_{x}x^{t}
. After distributing the derivatives on both factors we find terms

\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{1}}(\partial^{2}_{xx}a(x^{t},\xi^{t}))\cdot(\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{2}}\partial_{x}x^{t})

with conditions

|\alpha_{1}|+|\alpha_{2}|=|\alpha|,\quad|\beta|=|\beta_{1}|+|\beta_{2}|,\quad|\alpha_{2}|+|\beta_{2}|<|\alpha|+|\beta|.

The first two constraints are due to Leibniz’s rule, the last one reflects that not all derivatives act on
\partial_{x}x^{t}
in the inhomogeneity
\eta
.

We expand the first factor to invoke the induction hypothesis: after applying
\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{1}}(\partial^{2}_{xx}a(x^{t},\xi^{t}))
we find like above terms of the form

(4.65)

\partial_{x}^{\beta_{11}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{12}}\partial^{2}_{xx}a(x^{t},\xi^{t})\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{11}|}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{11}^{k}}x^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{12}|}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{12}^{k}}\xi^{t}.

The conditions on
\beta_{ij}^{k}
read

1\leq|\beta_{1j}^{k}|\leq|\beta_{1}|,\quad\sum_{k=1}^{|\beta_{11}|}|\beta_{11}^{k}|+\sum_{k=1}^{|\beta_{12}|}|\beta_{12}^{k}|=|\beta_{1}|.

We apply
\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}
to (
4.65
) to find

\begin{split}&\quad\sum_{\alpha_{11},\alpha_{12}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{11}}(\partial_{x}^{\beta_{11}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{12}}\partial^{2}_{xx}a(x^{t},\xi^{t}))\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{12}}\big(\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{11}|}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{11}^{k}}x^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{12}|}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{12}^{k}}\xi^{t}\big)\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{111},\alpha_{112},\alpha_{111}^{k},\\
\alpha_{112}^{k},\alpha_{121}^{k},\alpha_{122}^{k}\end{subarray}}(\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{111}}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha_{112}}\partial_{x}^{\beta_{11}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{12}}\partial^{2}_{xx}a)(\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{111}|}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{111}^{k}}x^{t})(\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{112}|}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{112}^{k}}\xi^{t})\\
&\quad\quad\times\big(\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{11}|}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{121}^{k}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{11}^{k}}x^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{12}|}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{122}^{k}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{12}^{k}}\xi^{t}\big).\end{split}

The additional conditions read

\sum_{k=1}^{|\beta_{11}|}|\alpha_{121}^{k}|+\sum_{k=1}^{|\beta_{12}|}|\alpha_{122}^{k}|=|\alpha_{12}|,\,|\alpha_{11}|+|\alpha_{12}|=|\alpha_{1}|,\,\sum_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{111}|}|\alpha_{111}^{k}|+\sum_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{112}|}|\alpha_{112}^{k}|=|\alpha_{11}|.

Having collected all conditions on indices we can invoke the induction hypothesis to estimate the terms as

(4.66)

\begin{split}&\quad|\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{111}}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha_{112}}\partial_{x}^{\beta_{11}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{12}}\partial^{2}_{xx}a|\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{111}|}|\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{111}^{k}}x^{t}|\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{112}|}|\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{112}^{k}}\xi^{t}|\\
&\quad\quad\times\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{11}|}|\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{121}^{k}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{11}^{k}}x^{t}|\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{12}|}|\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{122}^{k}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{12}^{k}}\xi^{t}|\\
&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\alpha_{111}|}{2}+\frac{|\beta_{11}|}{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{111}|}R^{\frac{|\alpha_{111}^{k}|-1}{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{112}|}|t|R^{\frac{|\alpha_{112}^{k}|-1}{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{11}|}|\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{121}^{k}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{11}^{k}}x^{t}|\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{12}|}|\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{122}^{k}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{12}^{k}}\xi^{t}|.\end{split}

By the summation properties of the
\alpha
-indices we can estimate the first three factors in (
4.66
) by

(4.67)

\begin{split}&\quad R^{\frac{|\alpha_{111}|}{2}+\frac{|\beta_{11}|}{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{111}|}R^{\frac{|\alpha_{111}^{k}|-1}{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{112}|}|t|R^{\frac{|\alpha_{112}^{k}|-1}{2}}\\
&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\beta_{11}|}{2}+\frac{|\alpha_{11}|}{2}-\frac{|\alpha_{112}|}{2}}|t|^{|\alpha_{112}|}.\end{split}

To estimate the fourth factor efficiently, we partition
\{1,\ldots,|\beta_{11}|\}
into sets
(a)
and
(b)
:

k\in(a)\Leftrightarrow\alpha_{121}^{k}=0,\quad k\in(b)\Leftrightarrow\alpha_{121}^{k}\geq 1.

We can now effectively estimate

(4.68)

\begin{split}(IV)\text{ in }\eqref{eq:AllTermsMixedRegularity}&\lesssim\prod_{k\in(a)}|t|(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta_{11}^{k}|-1}\cdot\prod_{k\in(b)}|t|R^{\frac{|\alpha_{121}^{k}|-1}{2}}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta_{11}^{k}|}\\
&\lesssim|t|^{|\beta_{11}|}R^{\sum_{k\in(b)}\frac{|\alpha_{121}^{k}|-1}{2}}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{\sum_{k\in(b)}|\beta_{12}^{k}|+\sum_{k\in(a)}|\beta_{11}^{k}|-\#(a)}.\end{split}

And moreover,

(4.69)

\begin{split}R^{\frac{|\beta_{11}|}{2}}\eqref{eq:AuxiliaryEstimateAllTerms}&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\beta_{11}|}{2}}|t|^{|\beta_{11}|}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{\sum_{k}|\beta^{k}_{11}|-\#(a)}R^{\sum_{k}\frac{|\alpha_{121}^{k}|}{2}-\#(b)}\\
&\lesssim R^{\sum_{k}\frac{|\alpha_{121}^{k}|}{2}}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{\sum_{k}|\beta_{11}^{k}|}.\end{split}

Taking (
4.67
), (
4.69
), and the fifth term in (
4.66
) together we find

\begin{split}\eqref{eq:AllTermsMixedRegularity}&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\alpha_{11}|}{2}}R^{\sum_{k}\frac{|\alpha_{121}^{k}|}{2}}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{\sum_{k}|\beta_{11}^{k}|}\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{12}|}R^{\frac{(|\alpha_{122}^{k}|-1)_{+}}{2}}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta_{12}^{k}|}\\
&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\alpha_{11}|}{2}}R^{\sum_{k}\frac{|\alpha_{121}^{k}|}{2}+\sum_{k}\frac{|\alpha_{122}^{k}|}{2}}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{\sum_{k}|\beta_{11}^{k}|+\sum_{k}|\beta_{12}^{k}|}\\
&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\alpha|}{2}}(1+R^{\frac{1}{2}}|t|)^{|\beta|}.\end{split}

The estimates for the fith term are crude, but since possibly
|\beta_{12}|=0
there is no overall improvement carrying out a refined analysis.
∎

We state the essence of the above lemma; on the unit time scale any derivative loses factors of
R^{\frac{1}{2}}
:

Corollary 4.9
.

Let
|\alpha|\geq 1
and
|\beta|\geq 1
. Then the following estimate holds for
|t|\lesssim 1
:

|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}x^{t}|+|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\xi^{t}|\lesssim R^{\frac{|\alpha|+|\beta|-1}{2}}|t|.

Finally, we consider time regularity:

Lemma 4.10
.

For
|\alpha|+|\beta|+|\sigma|\geq 1
the following holds:

(4.70)

|\partial_{t}^{\sigma}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}x^{t}|+|\partial_{t}^{\sigma}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\xi^{t}|\lesssim R^{\frac{|\alpha|+|\beta|+|\sigma|-1}{2}}.

Here we do not track the time-dependence anymore. Indeed, we have

\partial_{t}^{\sigma}(x^{t},\xi^{t})\big|_{t=0}\neq 0
. This leads to slightly inferior bounds compared to exclusive space or frequency derivatives because the initial data is non-vanishing in general.

Proof.

We begin by deriving the governing equations for the higher time derivatives. Starting from the Hamiltonian flow

(4.71)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\dot{x}^{t}&=\partial_{\xi}a(x^{t},t,\xi^{t}),\\
\dot{\xi}^{t}&=-\partial_{x}a(x^{t},t,\xi^{t}),\end{array}\right.

we find for the second time derivatives:

(4.72)

\begin{pmatrix}\ddot{x}^{t}\\
\ddot{\xi}^{t}\end{pmatrix}=\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\partial^{2}_{x\xi}a&\partial^{2}_{\xi\xi}a\\
-\partial^{2}_{xx}a&-\partial^{2}_{x\xi}a\end{pmatrix}}_{C(t)}\begin{pmatrix}\dot{x}^{t}\\
\dot{\xi}^{t}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\partial^{2}_{t\xi}a(x^{t},t,\xi^{t})\\
-\partial^{2}_{tx}a(x^{t},t,\xi^{t})\end{pmatrix}.

C(t)
is bounded like before
A(t)
and
B(t)
, and so is the inhomogeneity by the regularity of
a
. This yields boundedness of
(\dot{x}^{t},\dot{\xi}^{t})
.

We denote the
k
th time derivatives by
x^{(k)}(t)
,
\xi^{(k)}(t)
. By applying
d^{k}/(dt)^{k}
to (
4.72
) we find

(4.73)

\begin{pmatrix}(\dot{x}^{t})^{(k+1)}\\
(\dot{\xi}^{t})^{(k+1)}\end{pmatrix}=C(t)\begin{pmatrix}x^{(k+1)}(t)\\
\xi^{(k+1)}(t)\end{pmatrix}+\eta.

The terms from the inhomogeneity
\eta
take the form

\frac{d^{k_{1}}}{dt^{k_{1}}}(\partial^{2}_{\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}}a)\cdot\frac{d^{k_{2}}}{(dt)^{k_{2}}}\dot{\zeta},\quad\zeta_{i},\zeta\in\{x;\xi\};\quad k_{1}+k_{2}=k,\;k_{2}<k,

or

\frac{d^{k}}{dt^{k}}\partial^{2}_{t\zeta_{i}}a,\quad\zeta_{i}\in\{x,\xi\}.

For the terms of the first kind we compute by the chain and product rule:

\sum_{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3}}\partial_{x}^{\kappa_{1}}\partial_{\xi}^{\kappa_{2}}\partial_{t}^{\kappa_{3}}\partial^{2}_{\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}}a(x^{t},t,\xi^{t})\prod_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{1}|}(x^{t})^{(\kappa_{1}^{k})}\prod_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{2}|}(\xi^{t})^{(\kappa_{2}^{k})}\cdot\zeta^{(k_{2}+1)}

with

0\leq|\kappa_{1}|,|\kappa_{2}|,|\kappa_{3}|\leq k_{1},\quad\sum_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{1}|}\kappa_{1}^{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{2}|}\kappa_{2}^{k}+\kappa_{3}=k_{1},\quad 1\leq|\kappa_{1}|+|\kappa_{2}|+|\kappa_{3}|.

For terms of the second kind we find

\sum_{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3}}\partial_{x}^{\kappa_{1}}\partial_{\xi}^{\kappa_{2}}\partial_{t}^{\kappa_{3}}\partial^{2}_{\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}}a(x^{t},t,\xi^{t})\prod_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{1}|}(x^{t})^{(\kappa_{1}^{k})}\prod_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{2}|}(\xi^{t})^{(\kappa_{2}^{k})}

with

0\leq|\kappa_{1}|,|\kappa_{2}|,|\kappa_{3}|\leq k_{1},\quad\sum_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{1}|}\kappa_{1}^{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{2}|}\kappa_{2}^{k}+\kappa_{3}=k,\quad 1\leq|\kappa_{1}|+|\kappa_{2}|+|\kappa_{3}|.

We claim that
|(x^{(k+1)}(t),\xi^{(k+1)}(t))|\lesssim R^{\frac{k}{2}}
for
k\geq 1
. This is proved by induction. We find for the inhomogeneity, taking the estimate for
l\leq k
for granted:

\begin{split}&\quad\big|(\partial_{x}^{\kappa_{1}}\partial_{\xi}^{\kappa_{2}}\partial_{t}^{\kappa_{3}}\partial^{2}_{\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}}a)(x^{t},t,\xi^{t})\big|\prod_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{1}|}x^{(\kappa_{1}^{k})}(t)\big|\big|\prod_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{2}|}\xi^{(\kappa_{2}^{k})}(t)\big|\cdot\big|\zeta^{(k_{2}+1)}(t)\big|\\
&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\kappa_{1}|+|\kappa_{3}|}{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{\kappa_{1}}R^{\frac{\kappa_{1}^{k}-1}{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{\kappa_{2}}R^{\frac{\kappa_{2}^{k}-1}{2}}\cdot R^{\frac{k_{2}}{2}}\\
&\lesssim R^{(\sum_{k}\kappa_{1}^{k}+\sum_{k}\kappa_{2}^{k})/2+\kappa_{3}/2+k_{2}/2}=R^{\frac{k_{1}+k_{2}}{2}}=R^{\frac{k}{2}}.\end{split}

By the estimate for the inhomogeneity, evaluating (
4.73
) at
t=0
and induction, we find

|(x^{(k)}(0),\xi^{(k)}(0))|\lesssim R^{\frac{(k-2)_{+}}{2}}\text{ for }k\geq 1.

We remark that this yields the refined estimate for
k\geq 1
:

|(x^{(k)}(t),\xi^{(k)}(t))|\lesssim R^{\frac{(k-2)_{+}}{2}}+|t|R^{\frac{(k-1)_{+}}{2}}.

Next we turn to the estimate of mixed derivatives. The starting point is (
4.73
). We apply
\partial_{x}^{\alpha}
. We obtain

(4.74)

\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{t}^{k}\ddot{x}^{t}\\
\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{t}^{k}\ddot{\xi}^{t}\end{pmatrix}=C(t)\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{t}^{k}\dot{x}(t)\\
\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{t}^{k}\dot{\xi}(t)\end{pmatrix}+\underbrace{\sum_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\mu_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}(\partial^{2}_{\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}}a)\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\zeta^{(k+1)}}_{(I)}+\underbrace{\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\eta}_{(II)}.

The inhomogeneities of the first kind are readily estimated like above by expanding

(\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{11}}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha_{12}}\partial^{2}_{\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}}a)\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{11}|}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{11}^{k}}x^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{12}|}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{12}^{k}}\xi^{t}\cdot\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\zeta^{(k+1)}(t).

with

\sum_{k}|\alpha_{11}^{k}|+\sum_{k}|\alpha_{12}^{k}|=|\alpha_{1}|,\quad|\alpha_{2}|<k.

We obtain by the previous Lemmas and the induction assumption that

\begin{split}|\cdot|&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\alpha_{11}|}{2}}R^{\frac{\sum_{k}|\alpha_{11}^{k}|-1}{2}}R^{\frac{\sum_{k}|\alpha_{12}^{k}|-1}{2}}R^{\frac{|\alpha_{2}|+k-1}{2}}\\
&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\alpha_{11}^{k}|+\sum_{k}|\alpha_{12}^{k}|}{2}+\frac{|\alpha_{2}|+k-1}{2}}=R^{\frac{|\alpha_{1}|+|\alpha_{2}|+k-1}{2}}=R^{\frac{|\alpha|+k-1}{2}}.\end{split}

For the second term we find in the “worst” case:

\begin{split}&\quad\partial_{x}^{\alpha}(\partial_{x}^{\kappa_{1}}\partial_{\xi}^{\kappa_{2}}\partial_{t}^{\kappa_{3}}\partial^{2}_{xx}a(x^{t},t,\xi^{t})\prod_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{1}|}(x^{t})^{\kappa_{1}^{k}}\prod_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{2}|}(\xi^{t})^{\kappa_{2}^{k}}\cdot\zeta^{(k_{2}+1)})\\
&=\sum_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}\big[\partial_{x}^{\kappa_{1}}\partial_{\xi}^{\kappa_{2}}\partial_{t}^{\kappa_{3}}\partial^{2}_{xx}a(t,x^{t},\xi^{t})\prod(x^{t})^{\kappa_{1}^{k}}\prod(\xi^{t})^{\kappa_{2}^{k}}\big]\cdot\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\zeta^{(k_{2}+1)}\\
&=\sum_{\alpha_{11},\alpha_{12}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{111}}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha_{112}}\partial_{x}^{\kappa_{1}}\partial_{\xi}^{\kappa_{2}}\partial_{t}^{\kappa_{3}}\partial^{2}_{xx}a(x^{t},t,\xi^{t})\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{111}|}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{111}^{k}}x^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{112}|}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{112}^{k}}\xi^{t}\\
&\quad\quad\times\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{12}}\big[\big(\prod_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{1}|}(x^{t})^{\kappa_{1}^{k}}\big(\prod_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{2}|}(\xi^{t})^{\kappa_{2}^{k}}\big)\big]\big(\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\zeta^{(k_{2}+1)}\big)\\
&=\sum_{\alpha_{11},\alpha_{12}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{111}}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha_{112}}\partial_{x}^{\kappa_{1}}\partial_{\xi}^{\kappa_{2}}\partial_{t}^{\kappa_{3}}\partial^{2}_{xx}a(x^{t},t,\xi^{t})\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{111}|}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{111}^{k}}x^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{112}|}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{112}^{k}}\xi^{t}\\
&\quad\quad\times\big[\big(\prod_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{1}|}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{121}^{k}}(x^{t})^{(\kappa_{1}^{k})}\big(\prod_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{2}|}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{122}^{k}}(\xi^{t})^{(\kappa_{2}^{k})}\big)\big]\big(\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\zeta^{(k_{2}+1)}\big).\end{split}

with

•

\sum_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{111}|}|\alpha_{111}^{k}|+\sum_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{112}|}|\alpha_{112}^{k}|=|\alpha_{11}|
,

•

\sum_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{1}|}|\alpha_{121}^{k}|+\sum_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{2}|}|\alpha_{122}^{k}|=|\alpha_{12}|
,

•

|\alpha_{11}|+|\alpha_{12}|+|\alpha_{2}|=|\alpha|
.

By the induction assumption and the previously established lemmas we find

\begin{split}|\cdot|&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\alpha_{111}|+|\kappa_{1}|+|\kappa_{3}|}{2}}R^{\sum_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{111}|}\frac{|\alpha_{111}|-1}{2}}R^{\sum_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{112}|}\frac{|\alpha_{112}^{k}|-1}{2}}\\
&\quad\quad\times R^{\sum_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{1}|}\frac{|\alpha_{121}^{k}|+|\kappa_{1}^{k}|-1}{2}}R^{\sum_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{2}|}\frac{|\alpha_{122}^{k}|+|\kappa_{2}^{k}|-1}{2}}R^{\frac{|\alpha_{2}|+k_{2}}{2}}\\
&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\alpha_{11}|}{2}+\frac{|\kappa_{1}|+|\kappa_{3}|}{2}}R^{\frac{|\alpha_{12}|}{2}}R^{\frac{k_{1}}{2}-\frac{|\kappa_{1}|}{2}}R^{\frac{|\alpha_{2}|}{2}+\frac{k_{2}}{2}}\\
&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\alpha_{1}|+|\alpha_{2}|+k_{1}+k_{2}}{2}}=R^{\frac{|\alpha|+k}{2}},\end{split}

which establishes the claim (
4.70
) for
\beta=0
.

To show the claim for mixed derivatives in
x,\xi,t
we apply
\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}
to (
4.74
) to find

\begin{split}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{t}^{k}\ddot{x}^{t}\\
\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{t}^{k}\ddot{\xi}^{t}\end{pmatrix}&=C(t)\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{t}^{k}\dot{x}^{t}\\
\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{t}^{k}\dot{\xi}^{t}\end{pmatrix}+\underbrace{\sum_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}\mu_{\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{1}}(\partial^{2}_{\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}}a)\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{2}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\zeta^{(k+1)}}_{(I)}\\
&\quad+\underbrace{\sum_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}\nu_{\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{1}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}(\partial^{2}_{\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}}a)\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{2}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\zeta^{(k+1)}}_{(II)}+\underbrace{\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\eta}_{(III)}.\end{split}

For the estimate of the first term we expand

(I)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{11},\beta_{12},\\
\beta_{11}^{k},\beta_{12}^{k}\end{subarray}}\mu_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\alpha}\partial_{x}^{\beta_{11}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{12}}\partial^{2}_{\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}}a\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{11}|}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{11}^{k}}x^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{12}|}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{2}^{k}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{2}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\zeta^{(k+1)}

with
\beta=\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}
,
\sum_{k=1}^{|\beta_{11}|}|\beta_{11}^{k}|+\sum_{k=1}^{|\beta_{12}|}|\beta_{12}^{k}|=|\beta_{1}|\geq 1
. We find

\begin{split}|(I)|_{\beta}&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\beta_{11}|}{2}}R^{\sum_{k=1}^{|\beta_{11}|}\frac{|\beta_{11}^{k}|-1}{2}}R^{\sum_{k=1}^{|\beta_{12}|}\frac{|\beta_{12}^{k}|-1}{2}}R^{\frac{|\beta_{2}|+|\alpha|+k}{2}}\\
&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\beta_{1}|+|\beta_{2}|+|\alpha|+k}{2}}=R^{\frac{|\beta|+|\alpha|+k}{2}}.\end{split}

For the estimate of the second term we expand the first factor as

\begin{split}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{1}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}\partial^{2}_{\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}}a&=\sum_{\alpha_{11},\alpha_{12}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{1}}\big[\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{11}}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha_{12}}\partial^{2}_{\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}}a\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{11}|}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{11}^{k}}x^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{12}|}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{12}^{k}}\xi^{t}\big]\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{11},\alpha_{12},\\
\beta_{111},\beta_{112}\end{subarray}}\partial_{x}^{\beta_{111}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{112}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{11}}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha_{12}}\partial^{2}_{\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}}a\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{111}|}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{111}^{k}}x^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{112}|}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{112}^{k}}\xi^{t}\\
&\quad\times\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{12}}\big[\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{11}|}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{11}^{k}}x^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{12}|}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{12}^{k}}\xi^{t}\big]\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{11},\alpha_{12},\\
\beta_{111},\beta_{112}\end{subarray}}\partial_{x}^{\beta_{111}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{112}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{11}}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha_{12}}\partial^{2}_{\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}}a\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{111}|}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{111}^{k}}x^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{112}|}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{112}^{k}}\xi^{t}\\
&\quad\quad\times\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{11}|}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{121}^{k}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{11}^{k}}x^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{12}|}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{122}^{k}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{12}^{k}}\xi^{t}.\end{split}

The conditions on the indices are

•

\sum_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{11}|}|\alpha_{11}^{k}|+\sum_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{12}|}|\alpha_{12}^{k}|=|\alpha_{1}|
,

•

\sum_{k=1}^{|\beta_{111}|}|\beta_{111}^{k}|+\sum_{k=1}^{|\beta_{112}|}|\beta_{112}^{k}|=|\beta_{11}|
,

•

\sum_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{111}|}|\beta_{121}^{k}|+\sum_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{12}|}|\beta_{122}^{k}|=|\beta_{112}|
.

With these at hand, we can estimate

\begin{split}|\cdot|&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\beta_{111}|}{2}+\frac{|\alpha_{111}|}{2}}R^{\sum_{k=1}^{|\beta_{111}|}\frac{|\beta_{111}^{k}|-1}{2}}R^{\sum_{k=1}^{|\beta_{112}|}\frac{|\beta_{112}^{k}|-1}{2}}R^{\sum_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{11}|}\frac{|\beta_{121}^{k}|+|\alpha_{11}^{k}|-1}{2}}\\
&\quad\quad\times R^{\sum_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{12}|}\frac{|\beta_{122}^{k}|+|\alpha_{12}^{k}|-1}{2}}\\
&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\beta_{11}|}{2}+\frac{|\alpha_{11}|}{2}}R^{\sum_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{11}|}\frac{|\beta_{121}^{k}|+|\alpha_{11}^{k}|-1}{2}}R^{\sum_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{12}|}\frac{|\beta_{122}^{k}|+|\alpha_{12}^{k}|-1}{2}}\\
&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\beta_{11}|}{2}+\frac{|\alpha_{11}|}{2}}R^{\frac{|\alpha_{1}|}{2}-\frac{|\alpha_{11}|}{2}}R^{\frac{|\beta_{12}|}{2}}=R^{\frac{|\alpha_{1}|+|\beta_{1}|}{2}}.\end{split}

For the second factor we find by induction assumption

|\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{2}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\zeta^{(k+1)}|\lesssim R^{\frac{|\beta_{2}|+|\alpha_{2}|+k}{2}}.

Together with the previous bound we conclude the estimate of
(II)
.

We turn to the estimate of
(III)
. To this end, we write for
\beta_{1}=\beta_{11}+\beta_{12}+\beta_{13}
,
\beta=\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}
:

\begin{split}&\sum\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{11}}(\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{111}}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha_{112}}\partial_{x}^{\kappa_{1}}\partial_{\xi}^{\kappa_{2}}\partial_{t}^{\kappa_{3}}\partial^{2}_{\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}}a)\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{12}}\big(\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{111}|}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{111}^{k}}x^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{112}|}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{112}^{k}}\xi^{t}\big)\\
&\quad\quad\times\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{13}}\big(\prod_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{1}|}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{121}^{k}}\partial_{t}^{\kappa_{1}^{k}}x^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{2}|}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{122}^{k}}\partial_{t}^{\kappa_{2}^{k}}\xi^{t}\big)\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{2}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\zeta^{(k_{2}+1)}\\
&=\sum\partial_{x}^{\beta_{111}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{112}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{111}}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha_{112}}\partial_{x}^{\kappa_{1}}\partial_{\xi}^{\kappa_{2}}\partial_{t}^{\kappa_{3}}\partial^{2}_{\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}}a\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{111}|}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{111}^{k}}x^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{|\beta_{112}|}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{112}^{k}}\xi^{t}\\
&\quad\quad\times\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{111}|}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{121}^{k}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{111}^{k}}x^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{112}|}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{122}^{k}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{112}^{k}}\xi^{t}\\
&\quad\quad\times\prod_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{1}|}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{131}^{k}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{121}^{k}}\partial_{t}^{\kappa_{1}^{k}}x^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{2}|}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{132}^{k}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{122}^{k}}\partial_{t}^{\kappa_{2}^{k}}\xi^{t}\;\times\;\partial_{\xi}^{\beta_{2}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\zeta^{(k_{2}+1)}.\end{split}

In addition to the conditions on
\alpha_{ij}
from above, we have

•

\sum_{k=1}^{|\beta_{111}|}|\beta_{111}^{k}|+\sum_{k=1}^{|\beta_{112}|}|\beta_{112}^{k}|=|\beta_{11}|
,

•

\sum_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{111}|}|\beta_{121}^{k}|+\sum_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{112}|}|\beta_{122}^{k}|=|\beta_{12}|
,

•

\sum_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{1}|}|\beta_{131}^{k}|+\sum_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{2}|}|\beta_{132}^{k}|=|\beta_{13}|
.

We can estimate the single terms as follows:

\begin{split}|\cdot|&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\beta_{111}|}{2}+\frac{|\kappa_{1}|+|\kappa_{3}|}{2}}R^{\sum_{k=1}^{|\beta_{111}|}\frac{|\beta_{111}^{k}|-1}{2}}R^{\sum_{k=1}^{|\beta_{112}|}\frac{|\beta_{112}^{k}|-1}{2}}\\
&\quad\times R^{\frac{|\alpha_{111}|}{2}}R^{\sum_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{111}|}\frac{|\beta_{121}^{k}|+|\alpha_{111}^{k}|-1}{2}}R^{\sum_{k=1}^{|\alpha_{112}|}\frac{|\beta_{122}^{k}|+|\alpha_{112}^{k}|-1}{2}}\\
&\quad\times R^{\frac{|\kappa_{1}|}{2}}R^{\sum_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{1}|}\frac{|\beta_{131}^{k}|+|\alpha_{121}^{k}|+|\kappa_{1}^{k}|-1}{2}}R^{\sum_{k=1}^{|\kappa_{2}|}\frac{|\beta_{132}^{k}|+|\alpha_{122}^{k}|+|\kappa_{2}^{k}|-1}{2}}R^{\frac{|\beta_{2}|+|\alpha_{2}|+k_{2}}{2}}\\
&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\beta_{11}|}{2}}R^{\frac{|\alpha_{11}|}{2}+\frac{|\beta_{12}|}{2}}R^{\frac{|\beta_{13}|}{2}+\frac{|\alpha_{12}|}{2}+\frac{k_{1}}{2}}R^{\frac{|\beta_{2}|+|\alpha_{2}|+k_{2}}{2}}\\
&\lesssim R^{\frac{|\beta|+|\alpha|+k}{2}}.\end{split}

The claim follows from integration in time.
∎

Let
(x^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}},\xi^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}})
denote a solution to the normalized Hamiltonian flow (
4.54
). We observe that
(x_{t},\xi_{t})=(Rx^{\prime}_{R^{-1}t},\xi^{\prime}_{R^{-1}t})
with
|t|\lesssim R
yields solutions to the original Hamiltonian flow

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\dot{x}^{t}&=\partial_{\xi}a(x_{t},t,\xi_{t}),\\
\dot{\xi}^{t}&=-\partial_{x}a(x_{t},t,\xi_{t}),\end{array}\right.

which was considered in Assumption
4.1
.

We record the bounds for the original solution:

Corollary 4.11
.

Let
(x^{t},\xi^{t})
be a solution to the Hamiltonian flow governed by
a
satisfying Assumption
4.1
. The following estimate holds for
|\alpha|+|\beta|+|\sigma|\geq 1
:

(4.75)

\begin{split}|\partial_{t}^{\sigma}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}x^{t}|&\lesssim R^{\frac{-|\alpha|+|\beta|-|\sigma|+1}{2}},\\
|\partial_{t}^{\sigma}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\xi^{t}|&\lesssim R^{\frac{-|\alpha|+|\beta|-|\sigma|-1}{2}}.\end{split}

We use the following to center the (modified) Green function
G
:

Lemma 4.12
.

Assume that
a
is like in (
4.46
) or (
4.47
). Then for
|\alpha|+|\beta|+|\sigma|+|\nu|\geq 1
we have

(4.76)

\begin{split}&\quad|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{y}^{\nu}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{t}^{\sigma}[\xi^{t}(y-x^{t})+\xi_{0}(x-x_{0})\\
&\quad-\psi(t,x_{0},\xi_{0})+\psi(t,x,\xi)-\xi_{0}^{t}(y-x_{0}^{t})]|_{x=x_{0},\xi=\xi_{0}}\\
&\leq c_{\alpha\beta\sigma\nu}R^{\frac{-|\alpha|+|\beta|-|\sigma|}{2}}(1+R^{-\frac{1}{2}}|y-x_{0}^{t}|).\end{split}

Proof.

We note that derivatives in
y
can be handled by linearity. We suppose in the following that
\nu=0
. Moreover, for
|\alpha|=|\nu|=|\beta|=0
, that is to say, considering only time derivatives, we see that the expression vanishes.

In the following we turn to the main estimate for derivatives in
x
,
\xi
, and
t
such that
|\alpha|+|\beta|\geq 1
. Let
|\alpha|=1
and
|\beta|=\sigma=0
. We have

\partial_{x}[\xi^{t}(y-x^{t})+\psi(t,x,\xi)+\xi_{0}(x-x_{0})]=\partial_{x}\xi^{t}(y-x^{t})+\partial_{x}\psi(t,x,\xi)-\xi^{t}\partial_{x}x^{t}+\xi_{0}.

The first term is estimated by Lemma
4.5
:

|[\partial_{x}\xi^{t}(y-x^{t})]_{x=x_{0},\xi=\xi_{0}}|\lesssim R^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot R^{-\frac{1}{2}}|y-x_{0}^{t}|.

For the second term let
F(t)=[\xi^{t}\partial_{x}x^{t}-\partial_{x}\psi(t,x,\xi)-\xi_{0}]_{x=x_{0},\xi=\xi_{0}}
. Clearly,
F(0)=0
and we have for the time derivative

\begin{split}&\,\frac{d[\xi^{t}\partial_{x}x^{t}-\partial_{x}\psi(t,x,\xi)-\xi_{0}]}{dt}\\
&=-\partial_{x}a(x^{t},\xi^{t})\partial_{x}x^{t}+\xi^{t}\partial_{x}(\partial_{\xi}a(x^{t},\xi^{t}))\\
&\quad-\partial_{x}[\xi^{t}\partial_{\xi}a(x^{t},t;\xi^{t})-a(x^{t},t;\xi^{t})]\\
&=-\partial_{x}a(x^{t},t,\xi^{t})\partial_{x}x^{t}-\partial_{x}\xi^{t}\partial_{\xi}a(x^{t},t,\xi^{t})+\partial_{x}a(x^{t},t,\xi^{t})\partial_{x}x^{t}+\partial_{\xi}a(x^{t},t;\xi^{t})\partial_{x}\xi^{t}=0.\end{split}

For other combinations of
\alpha,\beta,\sigma
we have
\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{t}^{\sigma}[\xi_{0}(x-x_{0})]=0
. We recast the remaining terms as

\begin{split}&\quad\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{t}^{\sigma}[\xi^{t}(y-x^{t})+\psi(t,x,\xi)]\\
&=\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{t}^{\sigma}\xi^{t}\cdot(y-x^{t})+\xi^{t}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{t}^{\sigma}(y-x^{t})+(\text{mixed derivatives})+\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{t}^{\sigma}\psi(t,x,\xi).\end{split}

The first term is simply estimated by Lemma
4.10
:

|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{t}^{\sigma}\xi^{t}\cdot(y-x^{t})|\big|_{x=x_{0},\xi=\xi_{0}}\lesssim R^{\frac{-|\alpha|+|\beta|-|\sigma|}{2}}R^{-\frac{1}{2}}|y-x_{0}^{t}|.

The terms with derivatives as well on
\xi^{t}
as on
y-x^{t}
are estimated by Leibniz’s rule and Lemma
4.10
. Estimating the second and fourth term is most involved. Let
